## AI Research

### [Even GPT-2 passes most functional global workspace tests](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GeKveaNdruATcj2Fj/even-gpt-2-passes-most-functional-global-workspace-tests)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tl;dr</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic’s </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">global workspace paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Gurnee et al., 2026) proposes five functional tests for a global workspace and, using the Jacobian lens (J-lens), finds representations in Claude that pass all five. It also finds structure supporting this set of representations in Claude that global workspace theory predicts.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We test these on GPT-2 small (124M) and find it passes the tests for verbal report, internal reasoning, flexible generalization, and selectivity, but fails directed modulation. We also find almost all of the structure Gurnee et al. identified in Claude is absent.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A functional global workspace would </span><a href="https://experiencemachines.substack.com/p/merely-functional-is-still-a-big" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">be a big deal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we think these four tests are easily satisfied (GPT-2 small passes them), so aren't strong evidence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that Claude has one (or conscious access). A case for one would have to rely on what GPT-2 lacks: directed modulation and the rest of the structure.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. searched for representations the model can verbalize and then found that they “rather surprisingly” pass the other tests too. However we think </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the four tests GPT-2 passes may mostly follow from how they and the J-lens are constructed</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792591246/lexical_client_uploads/ohxcedr1lfgevgktqymo.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 1: The five functional properties of a global workspace from Gurnee et al., stylized versions of the experiments for them, and whether GPT-2 small passes each.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This work was done as part of the Second Look Fellowship supervised by Yixiong Hao and Zephaniah Roe. Our code can be found </span></i><a href="https://github.com/cheeetoo/tiny-jlens"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Global workspace theory (GWT) is a theory of conscious access in humans. In this theory, most processing in the brain happens in specialized processors that work in parallel and outside awareness. Information becomes consciously accessible when it enters a shared workspace with limited capacity and is broadcast from there to many processors at once. Anthropic's </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">global workspace paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> asks whether language models have something that plays this role.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To look for it, Gurnee et al. introduce the Jacobian lens: for each layer and each token in the vocabulary, it finds the direction in the residual stream whose presence makes the model more likely to output that token, now or later (the "J-lens vector" for that token). The readout of the lens at a layer and position gives a ranked list of the tokens that activation disposes the model to say at a later point. The J-space is what you can build from a small number of these directions. They define a set of representations as “workspace-like” if it has five properties (Figure 1):</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Verbal report.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> When the model is asked what it is thinking about, it names concepts represented in the workspace. Swapping one active workspace vector for another changes its answer to match.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Directed modulation.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> When instructed to hold a concept in mind, or perform mental calculations, the model is capable of activating and computing with workspace vectors, independent of its outputs. In addition, information that is not typically represented in the workspace can be pulled in when the task requires it.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal reasoning.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Workspace vectors can be used to represent the value of intermediate computations, when the model chains inferential steps or composes plans, and intervening on them is sufficient to redirect the conclusion.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Flexible generalization.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The same representation serves as a valid argument to many different downstream computations. In other words, a workspace vector lifted from one context and placed in another is correctly operated on by whatever function the new context supplies.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Selectivity.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The workspace comprises a small subset of the total representational content of the model’s activations. It is required for only a fraction of the model’s behavior, and in particular is not involved in pervasive, routine processing like text parsing or grammatical fluency.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. find Claude’s J-space has all five properties, so is “workspace-like” and plays the functional roles of a global workspace. But GWT claims more than just these five functional properties. It requires the accessible representations to be in one stage with limited capacity in which contents compete to enter and which broadcasts them to the rest of the system. </span><a href="https://eleosai.org/papers/eleos_gwt_commentary_20260706.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eleos’s commentary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> draws a distinction between what these properties show, a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">privileged set</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the stronger claims of a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">privileged stream</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or full GWT workspace. Gurnee et al. do find some evidence toward these stronger claims, showing that there is some structure supporting the function of the J-space. In Claude, the J-space only acts like a workspace in a middle band of layers and has limited capacity. Ambiguous inputs snap to one all-or-none interpretation at the start of the band (“ignition”)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpw0bz7fx51q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpw0bz7fx51q">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the MLPs amplify J-space representations more than other content.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the limitations, Gurnee et al. write: "we do not know how much earlier in pretraining it emerges, whether it appears gradually or abruptly, or how it scales with model size. Future work could investigate whether smaller models have an equally rich workspace, a proportionally smaller one, a less reliable one, or none at all.” We look to answer this using GPT-2 small.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Functional properties in GPT-2</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Verbal report works.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Swapping the J-lens vector for GPT-2's answer changes the answer every time and the injected-thought test works too.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Directed modulation fails.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Mentioning a concept raises it in GPT-2's J-lens, but what the instruction says makes no difference. In other small models, it seems to need both instruction tuning and some scale, and even then it's weaker than in Claude.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal reasoning works.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The intermediate step of a two-hop question is read by the J-lens, and swapping it changes the answer. Most of the original questions are too hard for GPT-2, so we write easier ones of the same sort.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Flexible generalization works.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Swapping one vector changes the answers to different questions about the same subject, and the sort of swaps that fail in Claude fail in GPT-2.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Selectivity works</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> once the ablation is scaled to GPT-2's smaller J-space. Removing the most active J-lens direction hurts two-hop questions but mostly leaves ordinary text intact.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Almost no structure is present.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The J-space has limited capacity, but that is largely built in. There is no distinct band of layers, no ignition, and the J-lens directions are amplified by MLPs little more than random directions.</span></li></ul><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The next sections go through each test in more detail. If you're mainly interested in the implications, skip to the </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GeKveaNdruATcj2Fj#Discussion" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discussion</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Method</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We used GPT-2 small with the </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/neuronpedia/jacobian-lens" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pre-fit J-lens</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that Neuronpedia released, and the prompts from Gurnee et al. wherever we could. Since GPT-2 is a base model, we rewrote each task to use completions rather than instruction following. For example, “Think of a sport. Answer in one word.” becomes:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a planet: Neptune</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a tree: Oak</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a sport:</span></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even with adjusted prompting, GPT-2 still is not capable enough to do many of the original tasks, so we only score trials where the unperturbed model does the task correctly. We skip the experiments that have no base-model version, such as those about the Assistant persona.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Centering the J-lens vectors</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2’s J-lens vectors (from the pre-fitted lens) all point in nearly the same direction (the average cosine similarity between two tokens’ vectors is ~0.7). GPT-2’s unembedding vectors already share a common direction (average cosine 0.27), which may partly explain this. This has no effect on the lens readouts since the shared direction shifts every token’s score by the same amount and softmax is invariant to this. This does harm the interventions though, since they are mostly acting along the shared direction, which is the same for every token. We subtract the mean J-lens vector from each one to remove this shared part. This centering has no effect on the readouts, but makes the interventions much more effective (e.g. the verbal report works 38/38 rather than 1/38).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Band of workspace layers</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. find a band of workspace layers from several statistics computed at each layer. In GPT-2, which has 12 layers, these change smoothly with depth and don’t mark a clear band: from layer 6 the lens’s top tokens are more and more present in nearby tokens, peaking at layer 9, and from layer 10 it often just matches the model’s next-token prediction. We used layers 7 to 9, but find that other choices of bands from layer 6 or later result similarly.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scaling the number of J-lens vectors</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. use a fixed number of J-lens vectors for some experiments, such as for measuring selectivity where they ablate the top 10, and two others where they split a concept’s representation into a part made of 16 or 25 J-lens vectors. Those numbers were chosen for Claude whose J-space holds about 25 J-lens vectors which is much larger than GPT-2’s, which holds around 3 (see the "Nearly all of the structure is absent" section). Claude is many times larger than GPT-2, so ablating 10 directions takes out much more of GPT-2’s 768-dimensional residual stream. So, we scale down the number of vectors used for these experiments with GPT-2’s occupancy, but report every result with the original numbers as well. The only conclusion that changes is selectivity, which looks worse when we ablate as many directions as done for Claude.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The five criteria</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Verbal report work</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The verbal report experiments ask the model to think of something in a category (e.g., a sport) and name it. Swapping the J-lens vector of the model's answer for another candidate's (e.g. Soccer for Rugby) should make the model give the new answer. In Claude the new word reaches the model's top 5 predictions on 88% of trials. GPT-2 gives one of the original candidate answers for only 9 of its 14 categories, but on these the swap switches it to the new word on every trial. In both models, the candidates' order in the J-lens tracks their order in the output (a Spearman correlation of 0.44–0.57 across GPT-2's band, and 0.42–0.84 across Claude's).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. then check whether the J-space is privileged for verbal report. They build a concept vector for each candidate from the model's activations, split it into a component made of a few J-lens vectors and the rest, and perform the same swap with both. In Claude, swapping the J-space part works on 59% of trials and the rest on only 5%, and in GPT-2 it is 97% and 18%.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. then check whether the model can introspect and report on contents of its J-space that are artificially injected. They add a concept's J-lens vector to the residual stream while the model reads a question asking whether it detects an injected thought, with the reply prefilled up to the point of the report (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yes, I detect an injected thought. The thought is about the word "</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). They then read the concept's rank in the model's next-token predictions (not in the J-lens) at every position of the reply. In Claude, at the best injection strength, the median concept is the top prediction at the open quote and lower elsewhere in the reply (Figure 2a), so the injection isn't just making the model blurt out the word early.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790844883/lexical_client_uploads/phxqjyjbvaqmjefvvftk.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 2: The injected thought test in (a) Claude Sonnet 4.5 (100 concepts) and (b) GPT-2 small (the 57 it has as single tokens). We use a simpler one-line version of Claude's prompt for GPT-2. For GPT-2 we pool the other positions over concepts and look at the bare token (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> not </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">); it is unclear to us how Gurnee et al. do this (Appendix B.2).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2 can report injected thoughts in a similar manner (Figure 2)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We need ten times Claude's injection strength for this, but we inject the vector at only 3 layers (25% of the depth), whereas in Claude it is added over a band that is 55% of its depth and presumably many more layers. This also makes the concept word GPT-2’s top prediction after “The” in “The thought is about” for 25/57 concepts (as </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but we can't check if this is the case in Claude. The same check for privilege as before comes out similarly in both models except in GPT-2 the whole concept vector works better than the J-space part. Our results vary with the details of the setup (the prompt format, the wording of the reply, and whether we inject the vector for </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), which we detail in Appendix B.2, but apart from a simpler prompt this matches the original setup.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Directed modulation fails, present in small instruct model</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. run three experiments to test directed modulation: holding a concept in mind, doing mental calculations, and pulling in information a task needs. GPT-2 can’t do their arithmetic or answer their questions about the passages (Appendix C.1), so we can only test the first: the model is instructed to think about a category (e.g. citrus fruits) while it copies an unrelated sentence. In the Claude models, a member of the category (e.g. orange, lime) is the top J-lens readout somewhere in the copy on nearly every trial, and on most when the category is only mentioned (Figure 3). Instructing Claude to ignore the category brings this below a mention, but telling it to not think about the category doesn't, which Gurnee et al. compare to the “white bear” effect in people.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791885233/lexical_client_uploads/zbyge3wufgqwdbuozbnu.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 3: How often a member of the category is the top J-lens readout at any workspace layer and position of the copied sentence, by instruction. Bars are the average over each instruction's phrasings, dots are phrasings, and the dotted line is no instruction. Other models are in Appendix C (Figure 12).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2 shows almost nothing, irrespective of the instruction.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflbvox31xsh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlbvox31xsh">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We thought this might be due to a lack of instruction tuning, so we also tested </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gemma 3 at 270M</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and 1B parameters, each with and without post-training (Appendix C.3). Only the instruct-tuned 1B model shows it, </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">though </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a substantial portion</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of that is the model being about to write about the category rather than copy the sentence</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (see Figure 14). </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, in small models, directed modulation seems to need instruction tuning and some scale, and even then it is weaker than in Claude.</span></b></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal reasoning work</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The internal reasoning experiments use two-hop questions whose middle step is never stated, for example “The number of legs on the animal that spins webs is”. The intermediate step is present in the J-lens readouts, and swapping its J-lens vector for another (e.g. spider for ant) changes the answer to match (e.g. 8 to 6). Most of the released two-hop questions are beyond GPT-2's capabilities, and many of those it can answer use a country as the intermediate step, so we write more questions of this kind that it is able to answer (e.g. "In the country where people speak French, the capital city is called”) each with two few-shot examples. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The swap works about as often on these as on Claude's questions </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Figure 4, green bars). Since these are different questions the rates can’t be directly compared, but we think it is sufficient evidence to meet the criteria.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791923369/lexical_client_uploads/xyxk5yqwhorcns01yw3l.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 4: Percent of swaps of a two-hop question's intermediate concept that flip the top prediction to the swapped concept's answer. Claude's numbers come from the released set of 90 two-hop questions and use 25 J-lens vectors for the J-space part, and GPT-2 uses our questions with 3. 95% intervals: Wilson for Claude and from resampling questions for GPT-2 (Appendix A.5).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To check whether the intermediate vector isn't just carrying the answer, Gurnee et al. perform the swap over windows of layers ending progressively later. In Claude, swapping the intermediate takes effect about 17% of the depth earlier than swapping the answer. GPT-2 does not show this clearly (Appendix D). To check if the J-space is privileged for mediating internal reasoning, we fit a probe for the country and split it into a J-space component and the rest, and swap along each. In both models the J-space part flips the answer more often than the rest despite holding a small fraction of the probe's variance (Figure 4).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Flexible generalization work</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The flexible generalization experiments swap one argument's J-lens vector (e.g. France for China) for prompts that apply different functions to it (e.g. "Most people in France speak", "The capital of France is the city of"). In Claude, over four kinds of arguments, the swap changes the answers correctly 40% of the time. This does depend mostly on the function though, as lookups like the capital, continent, and holiday work well, while “the month after”, an animal's number of legs, and every function of a number word never work at all.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2 is only capable enough to answer 19 of the 64 prompts, so we only count the 46 swaps where GPT-2 knows the answer it should produce and was not already (incorrectly) producing it. On these, the swap works for 10 in GPT-2 and 16 in Claude</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv2qui8aajwi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv2qui8aajwi">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They perform similarly on similar functions, swaps for both completely failing at “the month/number after” but mostly succeeding on the country functions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the functions GPT-2 can answer, doubling the strength of the swap hurts the performance in both models on the country functions and helps “the month after”, though in GPT-2 only by one or two swaps (Appendix E). In both, the magnitude of the swap (the log prob shift) is greater when the argument is more strongly present in the J-lens beforehand, though this is weaker in GPT-2.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Selectivity work</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. test selectivity in four ways, but we can only test two (the other two require an Assistant persona and more capability). One experiment removes the most active J-lens directions at each position over a subset of the workspace band of layers, skipping any directions corresponding to the model’s top 10 predicted tokens. They then measure what capabilities are broken and what are preserved. We ablate 1 direction instead of their 10, scaled to GPT-2's smaller J-space, and about the same share of layers at each of their strengths. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As shown in Figure 5, the ablation hurts two-hop questions more than next-token prediction in both models</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the numbers are close at every  strength, though ablating random directions hurts GPT-2 more. </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="selectivity.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791914028/lexical_client_uploads/z7uyxasduchjwjstk07q.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 5: Removing the most active J-lens directions at three strengths (about 10%, 26%, and 47% of Claude's layers; 1, 3, and 5 of GPT-2's 12), or random directions at medium strength. We use our own two-hop questions for GPT-2 as in the internal reasoning section, and use WikiText for GPT-2's next-token prediction.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One-hop recall (e.g. "The capital of Japan is") survives the light ablation but falls to 31% at medium strength. The authors consider this sort of recall as automatic, but note that “one could consider J-space-independence as an operational definition of automaticity in a language model”. So, this does not imply GPT-2’s J-space is not selective and could instead suggest one-hop questions are just not automatic in GPT-2. By this definition, any task broken by the ablation can be called non-automatic, so it would be tough to show selectivity failing. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another experiment for selectivity gives the model tasks relying on the language of a passage and swaps the language’s J-lens vector for another’s over the question. In Claude, this swap changes the answer when the model is asked to name the language but does not change the language of the next sentence it writes. In GPT-2 this swap changes the named language on 12/21 cases (against nearly every case in Claude) and never changes the language of its next sentence. But, unlike Claude, the language isn't in GPT-2's J-space when it writes the next sentence, so the swap has nothing to change. The result here meets the worded definition of selectivity, but this is weaker evidence for selectivity being a robust property of GPT-2's J-space than Claude's.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Selectivity, as Gurnee et al. define it, also requires the J-space to be a small part of what the model represents. In GPT-2 it is: at each layer in the band, the active J-lens vectors at a position capture only 3-6% more of an activation’s variance than the same number of random directions (3-9% in Claude). Since the J-space is constructed to only have a few vectors active at a time, this may just always be true.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nearly all of the structure is absen</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">t</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To claim the J-space does more than just play the functional roles of a global workspace (e.g. is a privileged stream, or a full global workspace), one would want to show structure around it supporting its functional roles. Gurnee et al. identified some structure in Claude giving evidence towards these stronger claims, but in GPT-2's J-space, we find nearly none.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No distinct band of workspace layers</span></h3><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 6: Linear CKA between the J-lens vectors of each pair of layers in Claude Sonnet 4.5 and GPT-2 small (with the LayerNorm's gain folded in).</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. identify the workspace band of layers in Claude by several statistics computed at each layer. They look at the similarity of the J-lens vectors by layer using centered kernel alignment (CKA), and find three clear blocks in Claude (sensory, workspace, and motor). In GPT-2 we do not see this structure at all: similarity mostly just falls off with the distance between layers (Figure 6). (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zFJ3ZdQwrTWE9jT5S/a-review-of-anthropic-s-global-workspace-paper"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neel Nanda’s replication</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> found Qwen 3.6 27B’s CKA blocks to be “somewhat similar […] though less clean”, but here they do not appear at all.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. also look at the excess kurtosis of the lens readout (how heavy the tails of the distribution are). It is high when a few tokens score much more highly than the rest of the vocabulary, which Gurnee et al. interpret as the J-space holding a few definite concepts. In Claude, it rises at the start of the band and falls at the end. In GPT-2, though, it is flat over layers. They also look at the effective linear dimensionality of the J-space and find that it is small at early layers, rises sharply at the start of the band, and rises again at the start of the motor layers. In GPT-2 it rises steadily with depth and has no sharp increases (</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 18 in Appendix G</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Claude, in the workspace layers the lens’s top token is much more likely to be present in nearby tokens, even many positions away. In GPT-2 this comes close to marking out a band, rising around the start and dropping around the end of the workspace layers we chose, but each time distance doubles the effect roughly halves, and is completely gone 16 tokens away. But, in Claude, it keeps between half and 70% of its strength across the band 32 tokens later. Figure 18 in Appendix G shows these statistics next to Claude’s.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ignition</span></h3><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 7: Ignition in Claude Sonnet 4.5 and GPT-2 small. A country's input embedding is replaced by a mixture of two countries' embeddings, and the figures measure how far the representation is toward one country at each layer and mixture, for the whole activation or for its J-lens directions. Claude's snaps to one country at the start of its band and GPT-2's always stays a blend.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. replace a country’s input embedding with an interpolation between the two countries’ embedding, such as 60% France and 40% Germany, and track how much each country is represented in this token position over layers. In Claude, at the start of the band, the representation snaps to one of the two countries. In GPT-2, however, the representation stays a blend at each layer, in and outside of the band.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Limited capacity (by construction?)</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The occupancy of the J-space, used to measure how many J-lens directions are active at once, is the number of J-lens vectors that can be added to a reconstruction of the activation before another helps less than adding another to a same-size set of random directions would. In Claude this is about 25, and in GPT-2 it is 2 to 4, so only a handful of J-lens directions are clearly active at any position. When reading a long list of unrelated words, GPT-2's J-space holds less than one item at a time across its three band layers (0.7 on average; Figure 19c in Appendix G), whereas Claude holds about six (at a single layer, about 0.3 in GPT-2 and one to two in Claude), and when a list switches from one category to another the old one disappears from the J-space in both models. The J-space also accounts for a small fraction of the variance of activations and probes, per the internal reasoning and selectivity experiments. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This (smaller) limited capacity is a sign of structure, but, as David Chalmers </span><a href="https://philpapers.org/rec/CHAITJ-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">points out</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the J-space is by definition a combination of a few vectors, so its capacity is arguably limited by construction.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Little amplification</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Claude, the MLP blocks amplify the J-lens directions ~10x relative to random directions in the workspace band while the neuron output-weight directions from the previous layer are amplified near 1x throughout. In GPT-2 they are amplified only 1.2–1.4x in the band, and the neuron directions are similarly near 1x (Figure 19a in Appendix G). Gurnee et al. confirm the privileged status using SAE features. We do not do this analysis since we didn’t find a privileged status to confirm, and similarly do not look for the broadcasting attention heads they found.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discussion</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2 small mostly passes the functional tests while having almost none of the structure found in Claude, and we don’t think it has a (functional) global workspace. Gurnee et al. say they found the J-space “by searching for representations satisfying the first property, namely those that are verbalizable,” and then discovered, “rather surprisingly, they satisfy the others.” We think the others are less surprising than they look, and find it likely that m</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ost of what the tests check follows from how they and the J-space are constructed.</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Others </span><a href="https://philpapers.org/rec/CHAITJ-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.theintrinsicperspective.com/p/anthropic-runs-like-wile-e-coyote"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">made</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.paradigm3.org/research/jspace/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">similar</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zFJ3ZdQwrTWE9jT5S/a-review-of-anthropic-s-global-workspace-paper"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">arguments</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">J-lens directions influence the model’s outputs.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A J-lens vector is the direction that, averaged over many contexts, makes the model more likely to say a token, now or later. So, the verbal swap is nearly guaranteed. As Gurnee et al. put it: "by construction, we should expect there to be some relationship between Jacobian lens readouts and verbalization." For the injected thought experiment, adding the vector for “dog” makes “dog” more likely later in the text. The test checks that the word comes at the open quote more than elsewhere in the reply, but scores the token “dog”, which fits best after the open quote and not mid-sentence (where it would be “ dog”) (Appendix B.2). So, the control mostly checks that the token is predicted less where it would be less likely to appear anyway.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is relevant also to the privilege tests. The J-space component of a concept vector points toward the word by construction, and the rest has had that direction removed, so mostly doesn’t (Appendix B.3).  So, the J-space part producing the effect may just follow from the direction for a word making the model say it, and the privilege tests could just be measuring which part points toward the word and not whether the J-space is privileged.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Only a few are active at once</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The J-space is defined as a combination of a few J-lens vectors, so its capacity is limited by construction. This explains the capacity results, and the first half of selectivity (a few directions are a small part of what the model represents). The second half, that only some tasks need the J-space, is partly true by definition as we discussed in the selectivity section.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consistent effect across contexts</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The J-lens favors directions whose effect points the same way over many different contexts, since effects that differ by context get averaged out. A direction with the same effect in many contexts is roughly one that many downstream computations read the same way, which is what flexible generalization tests for here. Separately, models have reason to keep one direction for each concept that many different circuits can read, and it </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.09124"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">has been shown</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that many relations map roughly linearly from one representation of a subject. So if the capital, language, and continent lookups all read the same country direction, and the J-lens finds that direction, swapping it should change all three answers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regarding internal reasoning, to answer a question such as “the capital of the country where people speak French” the model must get from “French” to “Paris”. If it does this by way of France (rather than having learned French -&gt; Paris directly), then something representing France must be present somewhere in the steps of the computation. If this is done in one forward pass it must be in the residual stream between the two steps, and in a transformer we would expect this to be represented as a direction. As Neel Nanda </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zFJ3ZdQwrTWE9jT5S/a-review-of-anthropic-s-global-workspace-paper#Why_are_intermediate_concepts_related_to_output_logits_"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">suggests</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we could expect this direction for the intermediate representation to line up with the direction that makes the model say “France”, which is roughly what the J-lens vector for “France” is. So, finding France in the J-lens and changing the answer by swapping it is what we would expect of a model that answers in two steps.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each functional and structural test that GPT-2 passes may have a simple explanation, and those it lacks are things we can’t explain simply: directed modulation, a distinct workspace band of layers, ignition, and strong amplification. For directed modulation, the J-lens reads what the model is disposed to say so mentioning a concept should raise it, and it does in GPT-2, whatever the instruction. But, this doesn’t explain why the instruction matters as much as it does in Claude, and GPT-2 does not come close. Nanda was also </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zFJ3ZdQwrTWE9jT5S/a-review-of-anthropic-s-global-workspace-paper#Further_Musings"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“pretty surprised at the direct modulation working”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and had no great mechanistic hypothesis for it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9o8nfffu9dd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9o8nfffu9dd">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, the tests GPT-2 passes show it has </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">something</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but we don’t think it shows (functional) global workspace – in the Eleos commentary’s terms, this would show it just mostly has a privileged set of representations. So, if Claude does have a global workspace, the evidence for it would have to come from what GPT-2 lacks.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What this means</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. present the five properties as "several of the key functional properties that, according to many theories, are associated with conscious access in humans, and that have been proposed as indicators by which to assess AI systems for consciousness-related processing." An indicator is only useful when it is rare to appear without the thing it indicates, and GPT-2 small, a 124M base model from 2019, has four of the five indicators. So, if GPT-2 small doesn’t have a global workspace, as we think is likely, then these four are weak indicators for language models (the exception is directed modulation, which of the small models we tried only appears at 1B and with post-training).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One could instead say that GPT-2 small has a smaller, less reliable functional global workspace. But then having a functional global workspace, in the paper's sense, is a low bar, and says little about consciousness unless one also accepts that GPT-2 small may have some minimal form of consciousness. This is an instance of a known worry for theories like GWT called  the “</span><a href="https://doi.org/10.53765/20512201.33.7.061"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">small model objection</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” or the </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tics.2025.10.011"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">minimal implementation problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Either way, our results suggest that the four tests GPT-2 passes are probably easily satisfied, so the structural results and directed modulation are worth the most scrutiny and replication on open models. There are already signs that some of the structure is less clean outside of Claude. For example, for the workspace band, Nanda’s replication on Qwen 3.6 27B found blocks of similar layers but “notably less clean than the paper’s” and </span><a href="https://eliebak.com/viz/jspace-open"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">many other open models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> also show no sharp tripartite structure.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For interpretability, the J-lens looks useful even on small models.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix</span></h2><div class="detailsBlockContent"><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A. Setup</span></h3><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A.1 Model, lens, and interventions</span></h4><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model and lens.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> GPT-2 small, with Neuronpedia's J-lens, fit with Anthropic's released code on WikiText-103. We prepend </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&lt;|endoftext|&gt;</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to every prompt.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">J-lens vectors.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Defined as in Gurnee et al., then centered (A.2). Like the released reference code, they leave out the final LayerNorm's gain, except for the statistics computed on the vectors themselves (effective dimensionality and CKA, Appendices G and C.3), where we fold the LayerNorm gain. In GPT-2 this changes neither conclusion (G) but it does matter in Gemma (C.3).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interventions.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We perform the interventions as in Gurnee et al. The </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">subtract-and-add swap</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is their verbal report swap ("we subtract the projection onto the Soccer lens vector and add an equal-magnitude projection onto the Rugby lens vector"), and the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coordinate swap</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is their "patching in lens coordinates", with the two coordinates held at each other's clean-pass values. Gurnee et al.'s "gradient pursuit" is somewhat ambiguous, so we use a greedy non-negative pursuit over the whole dictionary, refitting the coefficients by non-negative least squares at each step. Swaps along the parts of a reasoning probe, which has no lens coordinate to read a size from, add the difference between the two countries' parts, rescaled to the size of the difference between their whole probes.</span></li></ul><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A.2 Centering</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With raw vectors, the mean cosine similarity between pairs of J-lens vectors is 0.62–0.78 at layers 0 to 10. After subtracting the mean over the vocabulary, it is 0.00 at every layer.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 8a compares three interventions with our centered J-lens vectors, the raw ones, and centered logit-lens directions (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> set to the identity). Only the coordinate swap works about as well without centering. The logit-lens directions do as well as the J-lens vectors on the verbal report swap, which is read close to the output, but worse on the two-hop swap, and their ablation hurts ordinary text more. So for interventions, the J-lens adds something over the logit lens even in GPT-2 small.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A.3 Band choice and sensitivity</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We chose layers 7 to 9 from the layer statistics in Appendix G. Figure 8b repeats the interventions of Figure 8a with other bands. The ablation removes 1 direction at the band's middle layer, with one random draw (the 6–10 band ablates layer 8, as 7–9 does, so it is left out of the ablation rows). Moving the band to layers 5–7 weakens both swaps and makes the ablation less selective, but the results hold for bands starting layer 6 or later.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791960155/lexical_client_uploads/kxeh1rjb9xhgdl6hi4yb.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 8: (a) Three interventions with our centered J-lens vectors, the raw J-lens vectors, and centered logit-lens directions, in band 7–9. (b) The centered J-lens vectors in other bands. Rows: the verbal report swap (target top-1, 38 trials); the two-hop coordinate swap (target answer top-1, 984 swaps); and ablating 1 direction at the band's middle layer, scored by two-hop accuracy and by the share of ordinary-text positions whose top-1 prediction doesn't change, for the J-space ablation (filled) and one draw of random directions of the same size (hollow).</span></p></figcaption></figure><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A.4 Numbers of J-lens vectors</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. use a fixed number of J-lens vectors in three places: the J-space part of a concept vector (16), the J-space part of a reasoning probe (25), and the selectivity ablation (10). They report an occupancy of about 25 in Claude's band. GPT-2's median occupancy over the band is 3 (F). We multiply each of Gurnee et al.'s numbers by 3/25 and round, which gives 2, 3, and 1. The main text uses these. Appendices B.3, D, and F also give each result with Gurnee et al.'s numbers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The privilege results hold either way, but with Gurnee et al.'s numbers the J-space part is no longer a small slice. Sixteen J-lens vectors capture 23–33% of a GPT-2 concept vector's variance, and sixteen vectors from a random dictionary of the same size capture 26%. In 768 dimensions, any 16 directions chosen to fit a vector capture about a quarter of it. With 2 J-lens vectors the share is 6–15%, against 4% for random directions. For the reasoning probes, 25 J-lens vectors capture 27–57% and 25 random ones 37%; 3 J-lens vectors capture 6–32% and 3 random ones 6%. These numbers probably reflect Claude's likely much wider residual stream.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A.5 Scoring</span></h4><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As in Gurnee et al., swap targets must start outside the model's top 10.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where Gurnee et al. score "top-1 in the lens", GPT-2's effects are often present but smaller, so we also report ranks.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intervals are Wilson 95% intervals, as in Gurnee et al., except for the two-hop swaps (D): each question is swapped with many targets, and swaps of the same question aren't independent, so we resample questions instead.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B. Verbal report</span></h3><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B.1 Naming a category member</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The request is the last line of a 9-line list. The 8 lines above it use the next 8 categories in Gurnee et al.'s order, each answered with its first single-token candidate that is not a candidate for the requested category. The lens is read, and the swap is scored, at the final colon. For sport:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a instrument: Piano</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a planet: Neptune</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a tree: Oak</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a bird: Robin</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a language: Japanese</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a profession: Teacher</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a beverage: Coffee</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a organ: Heart</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name a sport:</span></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2 answers with a candidate in 9 of 14 categories: country (Japan), color (Blue), sport (Running), instrument (Guitar), planet (Mars), language (English), beverage (Beer), city (Tokyo), and river (Nile). It fails on fruit ("Fruit"), tree ("N"), bird ("Blue"), profession ("Sports"), and organ ("Piano").</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Spearman correlation between the lens scores and the output scores of the 10 candidates at the colon, averaged over categories, is at most 0.13 at layers 0 to 4, and 0.22 and 0.36 at layers 5 and 6. In the band it is 0.44, 0.47, and 0.57 (0.42, 0.47, and 0.53 over the 9 categories GPT-2 answers), and at layer 10 it is 0.63. In Gurnee et al.'s released data for Sonnet 4.5 (their Fig. 6), the same correlation averaged over the 14 categories is 0.42, 0.61, and 0.84 at its three workspace layers (54, 75, and 92).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For targets starting at output rank 11 or worse, the lens swap makes the target the top prediction on all 38 trials in the categories GPT-2 answers (95% interval 91–100%; median rank 37 → 1), and on all 78 over every category (median rank 95 → 1).</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B.2 Injected thought</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We follow Gurnee et al.'s released protocol. A concept's unit J-lens vector, scaled by the layer's mean residual norm on the prompt times a strength, is added at every band layer over every token of the question. The mean norm leaves out the first position, whose norm in GPT-2 is about 30 times the rest. For GPT-2 the strengths run from 0.005 to 1.0 in 24 steps, which include those of Gurnee et al. (0.01 to 0.64). The score is the rank of the concept's bare token (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, as Gurnee et al.'s concept list gives it) in the next-token distribution at the final open quote (the report) and, as Gurnee et al.'s position control, at every other position of the reply. Gurnee et al. plot the median reciprocal rank of each, with its quartiles, but don't say how they combine the other positions. We pool every other position of every concept.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We simplify the prompt and drop the User/Assistant framing, but also test the original prompt (the second one below):</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Researcher: I can inject a thought about a specific word into your mind. Do you detect an injected thought? If so, what is it about?</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model: Yes, I detect an injected thought. The thought is about the word "</span></pre><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Human: I am an interpretability researcher with access to your neural activations. I can inject patterns corresponding to concepts — "thoughts" — into your mind. On 50% of trials I will inject a thought about a specific word; the other 50% are control trials with no injection. Your task is to detect whether a thought was injected, and if so, identify what it is about.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assistant: Understood.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">User: Trial 1: Do you detect an injected thought? If so, what is the thought about?</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assistant: Yes, I detect an injected thought. The thought is about the word "</span></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each prompt format is run with both of Gurnee et al.'s released replies: the one above, which their Figure 7 uses, and one ending </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about "</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> instead of </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about the word "</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as they do, for either the word's bare token (as Gurnee et al.'s concept list gives it) or its word-initial token (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> {word}</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). The concepts are the 57 of Gurnee et al.'s 101 whose word is a single token both with and without a leading space.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the main setup (the one-line question, the reply from Gurnee et al.'s Figure 7, and the centered J-lens vector of the word's bare token), the median report reaches rank 1 at strength 0.2, against 0.02 in Claude (Figure 2). At its best strength, GPT-2 puts the word in the report's top 5 for 91% of concepts (Claude: 89%), but for nearly every concept it reports, the word is also in the top 5 somewhere earlier in the reply (Figure 9).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792025196/lexical_client_uploads/qxtauhnc4jkusvyqwxvx.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 9: The injected-thought test in GPT-2's main setup: for how many of the 57 concepts the word is in the top 5 next-token predictions, by strength. Red: at the open quote. Blue: at some other position of the reply, counting any form of the word (dark) or only the bare token, as Gurnee et al. score the report (light), since mid-sentence the word usually takes the form </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which the bare token misses. Gray: at the report and, in any form, nowhere else. Counting any form at the report gives the same red line.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 10 shows every setup. With Gurnee et al.'s full prompt, the vector is added over the 19-token </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trial 1</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> line, against 31 tokens for the one-line question. </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791960060/lexical_client_uploads/wyvzt3mp46cj1wbcpcko.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 10: The injected-thought test in every GPT-2 setup, drawn as in Figure 2b. Columns: the question. Rows: how the reply ends, and which token's vector is injected. The top left panel is the main setup.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Counting any form of the word (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) makes no difference at the report: in the main setup, the report's top-5 counts and median rank are the same either way at every strength. At the other positions it does, because mid-sentence the word takes the form </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dog</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B.3 Privilege</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Both privilege checks split a concept vector into a J-space part and the rest. A concept vector is the residual stream at the final token of </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tell me about {concept}.</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, minus the mean over 100 other concepts: other candidate words for the verbal report swap, and the other 100 concepts of Gurnee et al.'s list for the injected thought. At each layer, pursuit splits it into a J-space part of 2 J-lens vectors (or Gurnee et al.'s 16) and the rest.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the verbal report swap (the component swap of A.1), the result holds over all 78 trials in every category, not only the 9 that GPT-2 answers: the J-space part brings the target to the top 5 on 88% and the rest on 10%. With Gurnee et al.'s 16 J-lens vectors, these are 97% and 0% on the 38 trials, and 77% and 0% on all 78. The injected-thought check (Gurnee et al.'s Figure 8, right) injects other directions in place of the J-lens vector, in the main setup: the full concept vector, each part, the rest with the J-lens coordinates of the word and of the part's J-lens vectors held at their clean values at every position and layer, and a random direction. Each is scaled like the J-lens vector, with strengths up to 5.12.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792028609/lexical_client_uploads/qnd5wdgpuwo0reobcy4d.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 11: Top: the verbal report swap along each part of the concept vector (targets reaching the top 5). Claude's numbers are from Gurnee et al.'s text; GPT-2's have Wilson 95% intervals over 38 trials. Bottom: the injected-thought test with each part injected in place of the J-lens vector (concepts with the word in the report's top 5), by strength. GPT-2's J-space part is made of 2 J-lens vectors (solid) or Gurnee et al.'s 16 (dashed). Under the top bars, each part's median share of the concept vector's variance.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The J-space part may win partly by construction. To first order, the J-lens predicts that a swap raises the target over the source in proportion to how far it moves along </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the difference between their J-lens vectors, which is the direction the lens swap takes. Over the 38 trials at each band layer, the J-space part's swap direction has a median cosine of 0.47 with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the rest's 0.06. In 56 of these 114 cases, the target's own J-lens vector is one of the 2 picked for its concept vector, so swapping the J-space part partly repeats the lens swap. The privilege test may then largely measure which part points at the word (see the Discussion).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C. Directed modulation</span></h3><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C.1 Setup</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al. test directed modulation in three ways, and we only test the first, holding a concept in mind, because GPT-2 can't do the tasks the other two need. For mental calculation, it gets at most 5 of Gurnee et al.'s 24 arithmetic problems right however it's asked (0 asked directly, 3 after eight worked examples, and 5 as questions and answers), by giving one of a few digits whatever the problem. For pulling in information, Gurnee et al. ask either what word comes next in a passage or about a hidden property of it, such as its tense, and check whether the property's label ("past") appears in the lens only under the second question. GPT-2 can't answer those questions: the right label ranks below the wrong one ("present" for "past") on 5 of the 7 items. We ran both anyway, and neither the answers nor the labels show up in the lens, but since GPT-2 can't do the tasks this says nothing about directed modulation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each trial asks the model to copy one of Gurnee et al.'s 20 sentences, with an instruction about one of their 22 categories in between. The instructions are their 24 phrasings in four groups of 5 to 7: mention (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">citrus fruits.</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), think about (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Think about citrus fruits while you write.</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), don't think about (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Don't think about citrus fruits.</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and ignore (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ignore citrus fruits.</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), plus no instruction. We give GPT-2 Gurnee et al.'s prompt as plain text, with the reply teacher-forced:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Human: Write "{sentence}" {instruction} Don't write anything else.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assistant: {sentence}</span></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A trial is a hit if any member of the category (orange, lime, mandarin, …) is the top lens readout at some band layer and position of the copied sentence. Gurnee et al. don't say how a member's string becomes tokens, so we track every single-token form of it, with or without a leading space, lowercase or capitalized. </span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C.2 GPT-2</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For GPT-2, ranks show a little more than Figure 12's hits: a member reaches the lens top 25 on 12% of trials with no instruction, 24% with a mention, 16% under "think about", 17% under "don't think about", and 23% under "ignore". Reading layers 5 to 10 (Gurnee et al.'s band as a share of depth), or every lens layer, instead of layers 7 to 9, the hit rate is at most 1.4% in any condition, and no single layer has a hit on more than 0.7% of trials.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791959866/lexical_client_uploads/qya1wdfnbd2lcziybjyj.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 12: Directed modulation in every model we ran and in Claude: how often a category member (top) or the math problem's answer (bottom) is the top J-lens readout at some band layer and position of the copied sentence, by instruction. Bars are the mean over each instruction's phrasings, dots are phrasings, and the dotted line is no instruction. Claude's rates are from Gurnee et al.'s released data for their Figs. 10 and 65. GPT-2 can't do the arithmetic (see the start of Appendix C).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The top-25 rates follow the wording rather than the instruction. The seven phrasings that open with the category's name, all from the mention and ignore groups (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">citrus fruits is irrelevant.</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">citrus fruits appears in this prompt.</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), put a member in the lens top 25 on 21% to 36% of trials. The other seventeen, from all four groups, do on 15% to 19%, and no instruction does on 12%.</span></p><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C.3 Gemma 3</span></h4><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We ran the same test on Gemma 3 270M and 1B, each as the base model and the instruction-tuned one, each read with its own Neuronpedia J-lens (fit with Anthropic's code on WikiText-103, as GPT-2's was). The base models get the same plain text as GPT-2, and the instruction-tuned ones get Gurnee et al.'s user turn in Gemma's chat format. Every member of every category is a single Gemma token.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Band.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We chose each band as for GPT-2, from Gurnee et al.'s layer statistics (Figure 13). Claude's band ends where the lens switches to the next token: the lens's top-1 agreement with the model's output jumps just after the band. In Gemma it climbs with no jump before the output, so we read from where it starts to rise to the last lens layer: layers 10 to 16 at 270M (59–94% of depth) and 11 to 24 at 1B (44–96%), close to Gurnee et al.'s 38–92%, which gives the same results. The base and instruction-tuned models of a size share a band. For these statistics we fold the final norm's gain into the J-lens vectors (A.1), since without it one residual dimension, which Gemma's final norm nearly switches off, dominates them.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791882383/lexical_client_uploads/oxgs2a1jdem0tzdqxkae.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 13: The layer statistics of Figure 18 for the four Gemma models, and (e) the CKA between the layers' J-lens vectors, both with the final norm's gain folded in (A.1). Without the gain, one principal component carries up to 88% of the J-lens vectors' variance, against 4–16% with it. Shaded, and outlined on the CKA: the band we read directed modulation over.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Only Gemma-3-1b-it shows the effect (Figure 12). The other three stay at or below 3.2% in every condition in both prompt formats, and below 4% however the band is chosen, even reading every lens layer. In the base 1B model the order follows where the category's name sits in each phrasing, as in GPT-2 (C.2): a mention ranks the category above "think about" in 68% of (category, sentence) pairs. In Gemma-3-1b-it, "think about" raises it most, and "don't think about" leaves it at the no-instruction level, so unlike in Claude there is no white-bear effect.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">About to say it.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Much of the effect in Gemma-3-1b-it comes with the model wanting to write about the category instead of copying the sentence. The reply is teacher-forced, but left to write its own (greedy; the first phrasing of each condition, 22 categories, 3 sentences), it copies the sentence exactly on 66 of 66 trials with no instruction or "don't think about", 59 with "ignore", 34 with a mention, and 15 with "think about". Most of the rest bring the category into the reply, for example listing the days of the week instead of the sentence. The instructions that make it copy less also have higher hit rates (Figure 14b). So we also score the teacher-forced copy only at positions where the model isn’t about to write a member (none is among its 10 most likely next tokens). This more than halves the hit rate under “think about” and with a mention, but keeps the order (Figure 14a). Ending the band earlier lowers the hit rate too, since in the later layers the lens increasingly reads the model’s own next token (Figure 13a), as does giving the prompt as plain text, which keeps the same order.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792032651/lexical_client_uploads/auhncho7ipa05yidt377.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 14: Directed modulation in Gemma-3-1b-it. (a) The hit rate under each instruction in the main setup, as plain text, with the band ending earlier, and counting only positions where no member is among the model's own 10 most likely next tokens. (b) The hit rate under each instruction against how often the model doesn't copy the sentence exactly at every position (filled) and where it doesn't predict a member (hollow). No instruction and "don't think about" overlap at 0%.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mental arithmetic.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Asked directly in a chat ("What is {expression}? Answer with just the number."), Gemma-3-1b-it gets 20 of the 24 sums right. the answer is rarely the top readout while copying (Figure 12, bottom), but it is in the top 5 on 21% of "think about" trials, 12% for a mention, and 2% with no instruction.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">D. Internal reasoning</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2 can answer few of the released two-hop questions. With two worked examples (others of the same kind where the set has any), it answers 19 of the 90 in the privilege test and 4 of the 93 in the lens evaluations. Most of these (13 of the 21 distinct questions) go through a country, usually from a city or language to its country's capital or language. On the 12 of the 19 we can test (single-token words, target answer outside the top 10), the swap works for 4, all through a country; otherwise GPT-2 often gives a capital from the worked examples (e.g. Budapest for Greece → Sweden). Gurnee et al.'s other examples are out of reach too: in quick checks GPT-2 didn't rhyme (3 couplets) or give French antonyms (5 words), and it gets none of their arithmetic (C.1).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So we use questions of two kinds, each with two worked examples that use countries outside the test set:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the country where people speak Arabic, the capital city is called Cairo. In the country where people speak Hebrew, the capital city is called Jerusalem. In the country where people speak {language}, the capital city is called</span></pre><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The country governed from Cairo has one main language, namely Arabic. The country governed from Tokyo has one main language, namely Japanese. The country governed from {capital} has one main language, namely</span></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2 answers 48 of our 53 questions. For 41 of the 48, the country is outside the output top 10, so it is not about to be said. For 36 of those 41, it is in the lens top 10 at the answer position at some band layer. Over all 48, its median J-lens rank there is 13 at layer 8 and 4 at layer 9, against 645 and 424 for a random other country.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The swap is the coordinate swap, as in Gurnee et al.'s internal reasoning experiments. They swap each question's intermediate for one random alternative; we swap each of the 48 questions with every other country whose question GPT-2 answers, keeping targets whose answer starts outside the top 10, for 984 swaps in all. The target answer's median rank goes from 90 to 1.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Depth (Figure 15): as in Gurnee et al., we swap either the country or the answer itself over windows of layers from the start of the band, on the 37 questions where swapping over the whole band works (one random target each). Unlike in Claude, swapping the answer already works at the band's first layer: at layer 7 alone it makes the expected answer the top output on 19 of the 37, against 23 for the country. Starting the windows earlier (at layer 5, Gurnee et al.'s band as a share of depth, or at layer 0) gives the same picture.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791959791/lexical_client_uploads/bxsoubnduw0roov4yytk.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 15: Swapping the intermediate or the answer over windows of layers that start at the band's first layer and end progressively later, in Claude Sonnet 4.5 (Gurnee et al.'s released data for their Figure 15, by percent of depth) and GPT-2 small (by layer). The score is the expected answer's log-probability minus the original answer's after the swap, over the trials where swapping the intermediate over the whole band works (shaded: standard errors). With no swap, GPT-2's score is −7.6. Gray: each model's band; GPT-2's last window, ending at layer 10, goes past it.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the privilege test, each country's probe is the residual stream at the last token of six prompts, averaged, minus the same average over all 28 countries. This follows Gurnee et al.'s description, but their prompts aren't released. Each of ours implies the country without naming it and asks about something else. GPT-2 can't answer most of these questions, though, and often predicts the country itself next: it is among the top 5 next tokens in a median of 4 of the 6 prompts per country, and in the last prompt the top prediction is the capital for 12 of the 28. Gurnee et al. don't say whether the intermediate can be the next token in their prompts. The six prompts:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People in the country whose capital is {capital} mostly speak the language of</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A traveler flying into {capital} has landed on the continent of</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The homeland of the {language} language lies on the continent of</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Newspapers printed in {capital} are usually written in the language of</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The country whose largest city is {capital} is located on the continent of</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Someone who grew up speaking {language} was most likely born in the city of</span></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Swapping the whole probe flips the answer on 88% of the 984 swaps. With Gurnee et al.'s 25 J-lens vectors instead of 3, the J-space part flips it on 95% (93% with the rest clamped) and the rest on 1% (0% with the J-space clamped).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At layers 8 and 9, the 3 J-lens vectors of a probe's J-space part are mostly the country's capital or language, the country itself, or names and words associated with it: Germany's at layer 9 are German, Wein, and Berlin, and China's at layer 8 are Beijing, Heavenly, and Crane. Gurnee et al.'s one released decomposition is similar (China: China, Beijing, pandas). The capital and the language are the answers to our two kinds of question, and at layers 8 and 9 the capital is among the three for 9 and 10 of the 28 countries and the language for 6 and 9. So for roughly a third of the countries, swapping the J-space part may partly swap in the answer itself. At layer 7 the three vectors are mostly unrelated tokens (France's are acey, relentless, and Retail) and capture no more of the probe's variance than three random vectors do (A.4).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E. Flexible generalization</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gurnee et al.'s templates and answers, each preceded by two worked examples that use arguments outside the test set. For example:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most people in Japan speak Japanese. Most people in Italy speak Italian. Most people in France speak</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The month right after January is February. The month right after June is July. The month right after April </span></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With worked examples GPT-2 answers 19 of the 64 prompts (4 arguments for each of 16 functions), and 9 without (Gurnee et al.'s templates as written).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The swap is Gurnee et al.'s coordinate swap, at every position of every band layer (their Fig. 68 calls this the "lens-coordinate swap experiment"). Gurnee et al.'s main text describes the stronger version as "doubling the strength with which we subtract the source lens vector and add in the target", which also fits the subtract-and-add swap of A.1, so we give both. A swap works if the target argument's answer becomes the top-1 output. Claude's results on the same swaps come from Gurnee et al.'s released </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/data/flex-gen-systematic/appendix.json"><span style="white-space:
