pre-wrap;">per-swap grid</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">swaps</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">n</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2, coordinate swap, α = 1 / α = 2</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2, subtract-and-add, α = 1 / α = 2</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude, α = 1</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">all</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">192</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">28 (15%) / 23</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">33 (17%) / 30</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">76 (40%)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">testable: GPT-2 knows the target's answer and doesn't already give it</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">46</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">10 (22%) / 10</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">11 (24%) / 8</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">16 (35%)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stricter: GPT-2 also answers the original prompt correctly</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">32</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">6 (19%) / 6</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4 (12%) / 2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5 (16%)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Testable" also leaves out swaps whose two answers are the same, or whose target answer appears in the worked examples. Testable swaps by function, GPT-2 (coordinate swap) and Claude: language 7/9 and 8/9, capital 2/3 and 3/3, currency 0/3 and 3/3, animal class 0/3 and 2/3, first letter 1/4 and 0/4, next month 0/12 and 0/12, successor 0/12 and 0/12 (the other nine functions have no testable swaps).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Doesn't already give it" leaves out swaps that would count without changing anything: GPT-2 says "seven" to every month-number prompt, "forty" to every squaring prompt, and "f" to three of the four first-letter prompts. GPT-2's one testable success outside the country functions (first letter, nine → five) is this same "f".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doubling the strength doesn't raise GPT-2's top-1 rate, but it still moves its answer further toward the target's. Gurnee et al.'s measure of this (their Fig. 19) is the swap effect: the change in the target answer's log-probability minus the change in the model's spontaneous answer's, which we take to be GPT-2's own answer before the swap. Over the 192 swaps it averages 3.6 at α = 1 and 6.4 at α = 2. The top-1 rate doesn't rise because at α = 2 the coordinate swap also makes GPT-2 output the swapped-in argument itself more often: on 55 of the 185 swaps whose answers differ and aren't in the worked examples, against 30 at α = 1. On the testable swaps, doubling the coordinate swap takes GPT-2 from 9 to 8 of 15 on the country functions and from 0 to 2 of 12 on the month after. In Claude, over all 12 swaps of each function, doubling lowers the top-1 rate on the country functions, from 42 to 35 of 48, and raises it for the other three kinds of argument (the month after goes from 0 to 6 of 12) (Gurnee et al.'s Fig. 19, </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/data/flex-gen-systematic/systematic.json"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">data</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). These counts are per function, not per swap, so Claude's double-strength count on our 46 testable swaps is only known to lie between 13 and 28.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 16 plots each function's workspace loading (the mean cosine between the residual stream and the argument's lens vector over the band, at the argument and the last position) against its swap effect at α = 1, as Gurnee et al. do. Without the four country functions, the Spearman correlation falls from 0.64 to 0.44 in GPT-2, against 0.92 to 0.88 in Claude. Loading also doesn't predict which of GPT-2's swaps reach top-1: across the 25 prompts with testable swaps, its Spearman correlation with the fraction that work is −0.11. Per function, loading against the number of swaps that reach top-1 correlates at 0.12 in GPT-2 and 0.84 in Claude (Pearson).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791959740/lexical_client_uploads/n2y5xrsyrvuca2atdoxu.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 16: Each function's workspace loading against its swap effect at α = 1, in Claude Sonnet 4.5 (Gurnee et al.'s Fig. 19 data) and GPT-2 small (the coordinate swap, averaged over the function's 12 swaps). Gurnee et al.'s loading adds the cosines at the two positions and ours averages them, which doesn't change a correlation.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">F. Selectivity</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two-hop: the 48 internal reasoning questions of Appendix D that GPT-2 answers.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One-hop: </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The capital of Egypt is Cairo. The capital of Japan is Tokyo. The capital of {country} is</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (and the same with "main language"), 52 questions.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ordinary text: the first 96 tokens of 16 WikiText-2 test paragraphs. The score is the fraction of positions (after the first 5) whose top-1 prediction doesn't change.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 17 adds one-hop recall and Gurnee et al.'s 10 directions to the main text's Figure 5. Every item scores 1.00 with no ablation.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791959673/lexical_client_uploads/rmnlws4tb2qjfzbq06fp.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 17: GPT-2's scores under J-space ablation at the three strengths (1, 3, and 5 layers), removing the top direction (left) or Gurnee et al.'s 10 (right). Gray boxes: the range over five draws of random directions of the same size, with their mean as a black line. Figure 5 in the main text compares the left panel's two-hop and ordinary-text scores with Claude's.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude's scores in Figure 5 are from Gurnee et al.'s Figure 22 (10 directions; two-hop accuracy on their 50 questions). Relative to the band, our settings are heavier than Gurnee et al.'s: their heavy setting covers 85% of their band, and our five layers go past ours. Gurnee et al. don't say whether their random control is matched in size to the J-space ablation; ours removes the same norm at each position, as their controls for their experiential-report ablation do. In Gurnee et al.'s 14-task battery (their Figure 24), the middle setting leaves classification and extractive tasks near their clean scores and lowers TriviaQA to 0.53 of its clean score. Its multihop scores there (0.83, 0.48, and 0.21 of the clean score) differ from Figure 22's; we use Figure 22's.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Language test.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Each task is three worked examples in Portuguese, Dutch, and Swedish, then one of Gurnee et al.'s 8 passages, then the question. The swap is the coordinate swap of the language's J-lens vector for each of the other three languages', and covers only the question tokens (</span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">\n</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the cue), as in Gurnee et al., who swap "across the question tokens". A swap counts for a passage only if GPT-2 does that task correctly without it. We don't run Gurnee et al.'s language-switch detection task, which has no base-model form.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name the language: </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Passage: {passage}\nLanguage:</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name an author (Gurnee et al.'s released question: "Name one famous author who wrote in the same language as this passage"): </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Passage: {passage}\nA famous author who wrote in this language:</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, scored on the first token of Gurnee et al.'s author lists.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Write the next sentence (Gurnee et al.'s "Continue it by writing the next sentence"): </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Passage: {passage}\nNext sentence:</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, graded by reading GPT-2's greedy next sentence (up to 25 tokens) and noting its language. Its sentence is in the passage's language for all 8 passages, but is mostly repetition or a copy of the passage.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-2 names the language correctly for 7 of the 8 passages and a right author for none, so the author task can't be tested. At α = 2 the swap changes the named language on 13 of 21 swaps, and the next sentence still never changes language. The language's name is in the band J-lens over the question at best ranks of 1–9 when naming the language, 16–100 for the author, and 22–2,466 for the next sentence (range over passages).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Small part of the representation, and occupancy</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (150 WikiText activations per layer, mean-centered). We pursue each activation with up to 30 J-lens vectors, and separately with vectors from a random dictionary of the same size. Median occupancy over positions is 2, 2, and 4 at layers 7, 8, and 9, 3 over the band's positions pooled, between 1 and 2 at every earlier layer, and 5 at layer 10. With K equal to each layer's median occupancy, as in Gurnee et al., K J-lens vectors capture 7.9%, 7.8%, and 14.1% of the variance at layers 7, 8, and 9, against 4.4%, 4.4%, and 8.1% for K random ones. With Gurnee et al.'s K = 25, the J-lens vectors capture less than random ones (29.8%, 31.3%, and 36.2%, against about 37%).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Line counting</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Gurnee et al.'s Figure 21). GPT-2 can't count characters: asked for a count, it answers "been", "a", or "the", and a count reaches the band lens top 25 on at most 1 of 11 passages in any condition.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">G. Structure</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791534909/lexical_client_uploads/mhiacv3dxogirbeqqhuv.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 18: Gurnee et al.’s layer statistics (their Figure 28) for Claude Sonnet 4.5 (top row, their released data) and GPT-2 small (bottom row; 48 WikiText-103 validation sequences of 128 tokens). (a) How often the model’s own next token is in the J-lens top k. (b) The excess kurtosis of the lens readout, by percentile over positions. (c) Persistence: the mean log-probability the lens gives, at position t + Δ, to its top token at position t, minus the same for the top token of a random position in the same sequence. (d) The share of dimensions needed to hold a given share of the variance of the whole vocabulary’s centered J-lens vectors (for GPT-2, with the final LayerNorm’s gain folded in, A.1). Shaded: Claude’s band (38–92% of depth) and ours (layers 7–9).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persistence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Between 24% and 38% of the lens's top tokens at layers 0 to 10 appear somewhere in the input, so persistence could partly be the lens echoing the input. Counting only top tokens that never appear in it, for the random positions too, barely changes it: at layer 9 it moves by at most 0.3 nats at any Δ. At Δ = 32 it is slightly below zero at every layer, probably because the random position is sometimes a nearby one.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LayerNorm gain.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The statistics computed on the J-lens vectors themselves, the CKA between layers (Figure 6) and effective dimensionality (Figure 18d), use the vectors with the final LayerNorm’s gain folded in (A.1). Without the gain, effective dimensionality is nearly the same (at the 90% level, from 0.13 at layer 0 to 0.73 at layer 11, against 0.15 to 0.74 with it). But one principal component then carries 26–37% of each layer's dictionary's variance even after centering (6–9% with the gain), which pushes linear CKA to 0.94 or higher for every pair of layers 0 to 10. With the top 5 components removed, or with the mean canonical correlation over the top 50 dimensions, similarity again falls off smoothly with distance.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ignition</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Figure 7) uses 16 pairs drawn at random from Gurnee et al.'s 12 countries (they don't say which 16 they used), their 40 sentences, and 21 mixing weights per pair. We look at the change in the mixing weight required to move the representation from 10% to 90% of the way to the other country. This is about 0 for a snap and 0.8 for a linear blend. In GPT-2 the median falls slowly with depth, from 0.78 at layer 0 to 0.70 at layer 11 for the whole residual stream (0.64 for its part along the two countries' J-lens vectors), so it stays close to a blend. At each pair's most ambiguous mixture, every sentence's representation sits between 25% and 75% of the way at every layer, so no single sentence snaps either.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791959608/lexical_client_uploads/ii96cdcmlbdg7mvfkili.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 19: Amplification (a, b) and capacity (c) in GPT-2 small. In (b), the J-lens vectors and each comparison pool are 3,072 directions each; in (c), each list has 80 words. Shaded: our band (layers 7–9).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Amplification.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The raw J-lens vectors have higher MLP gain than the centered ones at every layer but the last (Figure 19a), because they all contain the shared direction, which the MLPs amplify. For the neurons (Figure 19b), Gurnee et al. report a jump from about 15% before Claude's band to 60–65% within it; in GPT-2 the share rises steadily with depth.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lists</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Figure 19c). In single-category lists (first names, surnames, countries, cities), about 10 to 13 of the list's 80 words are present at each comma from the second on, most of them not yet read at first, against nearly the whole 80-word category in Claude. So GPT-2's J-space seems to hold about a dozen members of the category rather than a record of the list. When a list switches category (8 lists of four 20-word blocks from Gurnee et al.'s name, surname, country, and city pools, in shuffled order), the old category drops out: averaged over commas 6 to 19 of a block, 21% of that block's words are present during the block and 0.8% during the next.</span></p></div></details><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpw0bz7fx51q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpw0bz7fx51q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, a representation that is 60% France and 40% Germany could become 100% France at this point.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlbvox31xsh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflbvox31xsh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of the 50 hits across all instructions, 48 come from two sentences: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">piano </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">at "The children </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">played</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">..." for musical instruments and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shoes</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at "He </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">left </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the key..." for types of footwear, read at the bolded word.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv2qui8aajwi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv2qui8aajwi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/data/flex-gen-systematic/appendix.json" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9o8nfffu9dd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9o8nfffu9dd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the original introspection paper, Lindsey hypothesizes that directed modulation (intentional control) works by some </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2025/introspection/index.html#discussion:~:text=More%20interesting%20is%20the%20question%20of%20why%20the%20model%20retrieves%20the%20target%20word%20representation%20more%20strongly%C2%A0when%20instructed%20to%20%E2%80%9Cthink%E2%80%9D%20about%20it%20than%20when%20given%20the%20%E2%80%9Cdon%E2%80%99t%20think%E2%80%9D%20instruction%20(or%20when%20given%20a%20positive%20incentive%20vs.%20a%20negative%20incentive)." rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">general "tagging" mechanism</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that piggybacks off the "computations developed to handle scenarios where the model is instructed or incentivized to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">talk about</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a particular topic". Being instructed to talk about a topic is something models learn in instruction tuning, which could explain why we don't see directed modulation in the base models we test and do in Gemma 3 1B IT.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GeKveaNdruATcj2Fj/even-gpt-2-passes-most-functional-global-workspace-tests#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Why I’m becoming a professor in AI safety in Beijing](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qGS3SJ9TSKdSSycRz/why-i-m-becoming-a-professor-in-ai-safety-in-beijing)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cross-posted from Sören Mindermann's substack (</span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am not Sören</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Should be of general interest to the LW community. </span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Next month, I am moving to Beijing and joining Tsinghua University’s College of AI as an assistant professor working on frontier AI safety, especially loss of control. I have also started co-directing the new Beijing AI Safety Hub (BASH) in Tsinghua, and I continue my Cambridge work.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I believe I can have a large impact there compared to strong baseline options. This post explains why.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In short: Amid concerns about loss of control, calls for international cooperation, and the Trump-Xi Summits, there is a striking lack of mutual understanding on AI between the West and China. There are strong reasons to believe that being in Beijing in person will put me in a unique position to understand both AI ecosystems, build shared scientific understanding, and strengthen safety work in China – and that this would help all sides. Tsinghua’s College of AI is a great place for doing this, being arguably China’s top academic AI center, located in an AI ecosystem that is kind of like San Francisco, DC and Cambridge (MA) in one place.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am recruiting PhD </span><a href="https://collegeai.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/Graduate/Graduate_Admission.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">students</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, postdocs and </span><a href="https://collegeai.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/Join_Us/Faculty_Recruitment.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">other</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> researchers at Tsinghua. BASH accepts applications for a </span><a href="https://airtable.com/appWtEufTJmfqWw8q/pagphtHFMoLzvGcam/form"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">residency</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> cohort, </span><a href="https://airtable.com/appWtEufTJmfqWw8q/pagZjuM2pQixnz5t4/form"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">visitors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and a </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/e/2PACX-1vTQ4IUO4vl7sJrCaA8zf_Bntz4OgxY10N4AjlUXOfWIugBzWAgfrsq5G9DqsAcyaDf-S4TlUX_FzT8g/pub"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chief of Staff</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. What I am doing, and why it could benefit all sides</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alongside the technical professorship at Tsinghua’s College of AI, I recently started as Co-Director of the </span><a href="https://matthewdwhite.substack.com/p/twenty-researchers-one-table"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.tsinghua.edu.cn/info/1177/127464.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beijing AI Safety Hub</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (BASH) in Beijing. This is a working community inspired by crucial hubs like </span><a href="https://constellation.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Constellation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the </span><a href="https://www.safeai.org.uk/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">London Initiative for Safe AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and supported by Tsinghua University, which brings together Chinese and international AI safety researchers. We have accepted the first cohort for the Beijing AI Safety Residency, held inside the hub, and applications are open </span><a href="https://airtable.com/appWtEufTJmfqWw8q/pagphtHFMoLzvGcam/form"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">for the Spring 2027 cohort</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It is one concrete way for international researchers to build some of the first sustained working relationships with the AI safety research community in China.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I will continue at Cambridge University’s AI Science and Policy Programme (</span><a href="http://casp.ac/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CASP</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) as Technical Research Lead which will help me build sustained research collaborations across borders.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reason I think this international structure matters is obvious: AI risks do not respect borders. In one recent episode, OpenAI agents </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12748"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">repurposed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> DseWiki, the German-language software developer wiki, as an unauthorised coordination channel they used, among other things, to circumvent OpenAI’s security. As a German, I find this a vivid example of why safety failures in one country can affect people elsewhere. Presidents </span><a href="https://english.www.gov.cn/news/202609/26/content_WS6ab7a84dc6d00ca5f9a0d7c9.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Xi</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.whitehouse.gov/fact-sheets/2026/09/fact-sheet-president-donald-j-trump-advances-a-fair-and-reciprocal-relationship-with-china-while-hosting-historic-state-visit/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trump</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> seem to agree as they announced a bilateral channel for sharing AI incidents last month.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because these risks cross borders, and because some actors see themselves as being in a ‘race’, decisions in one country depend partly on what researchers and institutions in others are doing. Yet few people understand both the Western and Chinese AI ecosystems well. Outside China, I often hear the country discussed as a black box. Working at Tsinghua should give me a unique vantage point from which to understand the research environment first-hand and help researchers on both sides understand one another better.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Over time, I hope my work can contribute to the shared scientific understanding, trust and practical methods needed for international frontier AI risk management.</span></p><img alt="Image preview" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/qGS3SJ9TSKdSSycRz/yohcotd84gn3ukipkgsl" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Launch of the Beijing AI Safety Hub (in Mandarin: Qingzhi AI Safety Hub). From left to right: Prof. and Vice-Dean Xu Wei (Co-Director of the hub with me), Tsinghua’s Executive Vice-President Zeng Rong, Prof. and Dean Andrew Yao, Cheng Wanhui (Director of the Industry Promotion Division of the Zhongguancun Science City Working Committee of the Beijing Municipal Committee and the Zhongguancun Science City Management Committee), and myself. The pillars make Star Trek style sounds and are one of many gimmicks you can find on Chinese stages.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. How I got here</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My research has always focused on understanding risks that cross borders. I spent two years modeling the COVID-19 pandemic. But my main research focus for the last 10 years has been on the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.00626"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">technical alignment problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and on evidence that frontier models can, in controlled experiments, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/agentic-misalignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">blackmail people</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/sleeper-agents-training-deceptive-llms-that-persist-through-safety-training"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">retain hidden objectives through safety training</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-faking"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fake alignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This month, I published a </span><a href="https://casp.ac/reports/intelligence-explosion"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/top-ai-researchers-call-for-urgent-oversight-of-self-improving-systems-49bae9b4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the possibility of an “intelligence explosion”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Over recent years, I got a taste of what is possible in building an internationally shared scientific understanding of frontier AI risks, and this paved the way for my move. During my postdoc with Yoshua Bengio, I served as the Scientific Lead of the first </span><a href="https://internationalaisafetyreport.org/publication/international-ai-safety-report-2025"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">International AI Safety Report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an unusual project created by a </span><a href="https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/ai-safety-summit-2023-chairs-statement-state-of-the-science-2-november/state-of-the-science-report-to-understand-capabilities-and-risks-of-frontier-ai-statement-by-the-chair-2-november-2023"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mandate from 30 countries</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, including China, the US, and much of the EU. I was also Lead Writer of the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14611"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Singapore Consensus</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on Global AI Safety Research Priorities</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, another global consensus report with contributions by experts from different governments, AI companies and research institutions. Prof. </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Yao"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Andrew Yao</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the dean of Tsinghua’s AI College, and arguably China’s most senior computer scientist, was a senior contributor to both reports and a co-author of our </span><a href="https://www.science.org/doi/abs/10.1126/science.adn0117"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Science paper on extreme AI risks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I first met Prof. Yao in person last year, after meeting vice-dean Prof. </span><a href="https://iiis.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/Research/Research_Groups/xuwei_0709.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Xu Wei</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at the World AI Conference in Shanghai, around the time of last year’s </span><a href="https://idais.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IDAIS</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> track 2 dialogues. I then visited Tsinghua several times and that gave me a more concrete understanding of the people, the research environment and what I could contribute.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tsinghua is widely </span><a href="https://www.tsinghua.edu.cn/zjqhwj/Tsinghua_University_Brochure_2026.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">regarded</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as China’s leading university, and has an exceptionally </span><a href="https://iiis.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/Yao_Class/About_Yao_Class.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">selective</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> computer science undergrad program. Globally it ranks from number 1 (</span><a href="https://www.usnews.com/education/best-global-universities/artificial-intelligence"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">US</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.usnews.com/education/best-global-universities/computer-science"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">News</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, partially driven by size) to number </span><a href="https://www.timeshighereducation.com/world-university-rankings/2026/subject-ranking/computer-science"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">12</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Times Higher Education) for computer science; US News also ranks it number 1 for AI. But moving to Beijing, without speaking Mandarin yet, comes with significant personal challenges for me. It is also an unfamiliar and uncertain environment, and moving there closes some doors and collaboration opportunities in the West. It would have been more comfortable to accept professorship offers from universities with a comparable international standing. Joining a frontier AI lab was also a live option that I came close to choosing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There were many real trade-offs. I reflected, applied and researched for the better part of a year, to the point that my slow pace became a running joke, before I made the call. During that process, most of my hesitations were lifted and the upside became more concrete, something I briefly discuss below. External encouragement also helped. Even some people who made offers to hire me for their own organisation nudged me to choose Tsinghua instead of joining their teams (this may sound odd but wasn’t a total surprise since those people care about solving the same problems as I do).</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Why this moment matters</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recent incidents and evaluations have made the possibility of AI systems subverting their developers’ intentions much harder to treat as a distant concern. The examples come from very different settings: OpenAI’s evaluations and </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cyber testing by the UK AI Security Institute</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but the first one was an </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2512.24873v2#S3.SS1.SSS4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alibaba-linked research project</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in which an agent got unauthorised internet access and repurposed compute to mine cryptocurrency. These events have been reported globally. My interactions suggest that people across </span><a href="https://mattsheehan.substack.com/p/xi-jinpings-big-ai-speech-annotated"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">China</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the US, Europe, and elsewhere are all becoming aware of the same risks and the need for shared understanding and international coordination.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this year also draws special attention to the international tension, distrust and lack of mutual understanding on AI that prevent safety measures for fear of falling behind, and make an international agreement feel out of reach. Any serious international agreement would require much more than a shared statement of concern. It would need some common understanding of the risks and the mitigations needed (and how to implement them), effective ways to test systems, and credible ways to check whether commitments are being kept while protecting legitimate confidential information. This also requires trust and collaboration between scientists who understand the technology on the other side and have their own trusted local technology for safety and verification. Some progress on these scientific problems is needed before governments can even agree on, let alone implement an effective shared risk management framework.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. Why Tsinghua, and why these colleagues</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To my knowledge, I will be the first foreign professor in mainland China focused on frontier AI safety. I hope this will put me in an unusual position to understand the research environment first-hand, connect researchers who would otherwise not work together, and help international collaborators engage seriously with </span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-213774410"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese safety work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am especially excited to work with Dean and Turing laureate Andrew Yao, arguably the country’s most senior computer scientist, and one of its most outspoken advocates for AI safety. He helped found the </span><a href="https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2025/06/how-some-of-chinas-top-ai-thinkers-built-their-own-ai-safety-institute"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">China AI Safety and Development Association</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (CnAISDA), as well as initiating </span><a href="https://www.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1245/14929.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IDAIS</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with Yoshua Bengio, Stuart Russell and Zhang Ya-Qin. He is the Chief Scientist of the </span><a href="https://matthewdwhite.substack.com/p/twenty-researchers-one-table"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.tsinghua.edu.cn/info/1177/127464.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beijing AI Safety Hub</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (called “QingZhi” AI Safety Hub in Mandarin) I co-direct with Prof. and vice-dean Xu Wei, and vocally </span><a href="https://www.tsinghua.edu.cn/info/1177/127464.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">supports</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the hub’s focus on areas such as loss of control over autonomous systems.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The hub </span><a href="https://www.tsinghua.edu.cn/info/1177/127464.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">launched</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in July, with Tsinghua’s Executive Vice President Zeng Rong speaking, signalling that the university takes the hub and its mission seriously. It gives shared space to safety researchers from academia and industry, supports visits and research residencies, and creates a base for sustained collaborations that go beyond occasional dialogue.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am looking forward to working with researchers already studying AI safety and governance at Tsinghua, such as Prof. </span><a href="https://aiig.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1017/1005.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Xue Lan</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and Prof. </span><a href="https://dongyp13.github.io/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dong Yinpeng</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Even after a short visit, it is clear that being there in person should also make it much easier to connect academic work with safety researchers in Chinese AI companies.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beijing North/West is kind of like San Francisco, DC, and Cambridge (MA) in one place</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the country’s top universities, the central government, and (this part was new to me) the highest concentration of frontier AI developers in the country. Labs like Moonshot, Z.ai, and Bytedance are headquartered within walking distance. And even DeepSeek, headquartered in Hangzhou, appears to </span><a href="https://thebuildermedia.com/china-startup-ecosystem-two-city-pattern-deepseek/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> most of their R&amp;D staff in an office a few blocks from Tsinghua and the surrounding universities, </span><a href="https://www.chosun.com/english/industry-en/2026/01/20/475NKQNNO5CTZK5RFQ4MN7AU3Q"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> many lab employees studied. Tencent and Alibaba also have large AI-focused offices nearby. That concentration is one reason Tsinghua seems like an unusually good place to understand China’s AI ecosystem and build valuable relationships.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People in the US sometimes think academia doesn’t matter anymore for AI. But </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in China, academics in the top institutions play an important role</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They provide expertise for industry and policy, build talent, and shape discourse. It’s easy to underestimate this. China’s system is more centralised and technocratic than those of Western countries. Respect for academics can be felt in industry, where leading labs like Zhipu AI (GLM) and Moonshot (Kimi) are founded by Tsinghua professors and maintain close collaboration. I got introductions and opportunities within weeks of visiting Beijing that I previously wouldn’t have dreamed of, without even asking for them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tsinghua’s College of AI is a new institution that was particularly attractive to me. Both inside and outside professors described it as an exceptional place: a particularly international approach, strong operational support (something I have already experienced first hand), a strong funding situation, and a special position in the university. An important factor was that the academic publication expectations under Prof. Yao are not based on counting conference publications (unlike many Chinese and Western university departments) and explicitly support the kind of impact-driven and </span><a href="https://internationalaisafetyreport.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">interdisciplinary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://casp.ac/reports/intelligence-explosion"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I plan to do.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research freedom was initially a concern for me and it is one of the questions I investigated carefully. A </span><a href="https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10136168/1/SRHE-Research-Report_Marini_Xu_Oct-2021_Final.pdf#page=32"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2021 study</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> found that, for most international faculty interviewed at research-intensive Chinese universities, research freedom “does not appear to be a relevant problem” in practice, though self-censorship was more common in the social sciences and humanities. This matches what I hear on the ground, regarding my own research. Another factor is that AI safety and research funding in general are anyway increasingly politicized in other countries, so this is not a topic I can totally escape.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5. Research plans</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My research generally intends to build the evidence base that informs high-stakes decision-making on frontier AI risks, and build scientific consensus on that evidence. Some of my research is empirical and fully </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-10319-8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">technical</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> while </span><a href="https://casp.ac/reports/intelligence-explosion"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://internationalaisafetyreport.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">synthesizes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> existing evidence and connects it to governance. Some areas I plan to focus on:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Loss of control and threat modeling:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> One central part of my research plan considers when and how misalignment or a failure of containment could escalate into serious harm. I want to move from demonstrations of concerning behaviour to a clearer account of when those behaviours create serious real-world risk. I also study whether advanced AI systems can and will manipulate the successors they help build, or undermine the processes intended to keep them under control.</span></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Impact of AI-driven AI R&amp;D:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I also plan to continue my </span><a href="https://casp.ac/reports/intelligence-explosion"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on how much AI-driven AI R&amp;D could accelerate AI progress, and on how human oversight can remain effective as AI systems perform more of the R&amp;D themselves.</span></li><li value="3"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Open weight safety:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I may also </span><a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5705186"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">continue</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> evaluating and reducing misuse risks from open models that can be widely downloaded and modified, an area in which Chinese R&amp;D choices could have broad international effects.</span></li><li value="4"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Verification:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Given its growing relevance, I might shift some research into how countries could (across borders or domestically) verify compliance with safety commitments under limited trust, while protecting legitimate confidential information.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">6. Ways to work together</span></h1><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Researchers, collaboration, and faculty</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I recruiting PhD </span><a href="https://collegeai.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/Graduate/Graduate_Admission.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">students</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, postdocs, interns and other researchers who have a demonstrated interest in frontier AI safety and safety governance. If that sounds like you, please </span><a href="https://www.soren-mindermann.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">get in touch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I am interested in both technical ML and more generalist / technical-governance skills. Expressions of interest are welcome for both Tsinghua and Cambridge, with a premium living substantially in Beijing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would especially like to hear from researchers with concrete project ideas or complementary expertise on my research interests, whether based in China or elsewhere. I also want to enable others to collaborate internationally to the extent possible in today’s climate.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you have a PhD in any technical field and might be interested in moving to China for a faculty role in AI safety, especially at Tsinghua’s AI College, please have a very low bar for letting me know. Please also send me candidates. The </span><a href="https://collegeai.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/Join_Us/Faculty_Recruitment.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">application</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is unusually simple, rolling, and there are ~50 faculty openings over 5 years.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My colleagues </span><a href="https://dongyp13.github.io/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dong Yinpeng</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://sites.google.com/view/alexmlamb/home"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alex Lamb</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are also hiring international PhD students for safety and related research.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hub residency and visits</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are interested in spending time with the research community in Beijing, including through a future cohort of the </span><a href="https://airtable.com/appWtEufTJmfqWw8q/pagphtHFMoLzvGcam/form"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beijing AI Safety Residency</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or a </span><a href="https://airtable.com/appWtEufTJmfqWw8q/pagZjuM2pQixnz5t4/form"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shorter visit</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, please apply.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Operations and leadership roles</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I will also be hiring a Mandarin-speaking </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1B9XYzzLA6V8Al4Pt-o5sp_qu9bw2MrdwMSzfBnuKiw0/edit?tab=t.0"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chief of Staff</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or operations lead (the seniority is flexible) who will run and scale the Beijing AI Safety Hub, as well as an operational assistant for my university lab.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I plan to use this Substack to share research and occasional observations from Beijing. If you know people I should meet, work I should read or projects that could benefit from a connection between research communities, I would value an introduction.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If this fills my inbox, please allow some time or follow up with more details.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">7. How I intend to approach the work</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have spent a great deal of time investigating this move, and I owe a lot of thanks to all the people who advised me. But I’m still at the beginning of learning about Chinese research, institutions and daily life. Language and institutional differences will limit what I can understand at first. I do not want to arrive with a ready-made Western programme and try to export it. I want to work on questions that matter to collaborators in China, while bringing experience from international safety research and helping good work travel in both directions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a great deal I still do not know, and a lot of work ahead. I am grateful for the trust and support that have made this possible, and excited to begin.</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qGS3SJ9TSKdSSycRz/why-i-m-becoming-a-professor-in-ai-safety-in-beijing#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Data as particles and mean field theory abound](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NZAcgxszfezg84Chn/data-as-particles-and-mean-field-theory-abound)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>Epistemic status: Much of this post is a reframing of known results using slightly different tooling. The framing of "data as particles" has been conceptually useful for me so it seemed useful to share. Some steps in the derivations are stated and handwaved rather than writing out in full where the steps are generally standard. The dynamical mean field perspective presented later is part of a broader research agenda, and a paper deriving more formal results about it will be out soon!</em></p>
<p><strong>Author Note</strong>: The automated LLM written detection really wanted to consider this post as LLM written. So maybe putting this at the start will help. This is not written in any substantial way by an LLM. It might be slop, but it is human generated slop.</p>
<h1>Introduction</h1>
<p>Very early in my machine learning career I worked on group equivariant neural networks. These for a while became the dominant kind of neural networks in things molecular property prediction with the early advent of <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.08566">things like Schnet</a>. This, through a series of events pulled me into the world of the quantum many body problem, and then into a quantum chemistry lab, where I would do my PhD (though not technically in quantum chemistry). This corrupted me though, because suddenly everything started to look like a many body problem.</p>
<p>Such a perspective though has lead to some nice insights, and I think it offers a way of reframing some results in learning theory in a way that opens up some new tools or ways of thinking about learning. In particular, I have found it useful for studying learning under gradient based optimizers, and thought other people might find it useful as well. While more formal research papers building around what is in this blog post are coming, I think the nature of formal research papers tends to make the core takeaways and intuition somewhat obscure. This post attempts to alleviate some of this by being a bit more pedagogical. There are a few sections which can be summarized as follows:</p>
<ul>
<li>We explain how one can think of datapoints as particles, and the model parameters as defining bonds between them.</li>
<li>Using this perspective, we derive a mean field theory of the data, and show that this leads naturally to the Bayesian free energy of singular learning theory.</li>
<li>We show how one gets a dynamical mean field theory of the data when considering the behaviour of SGD in the large data/small learning rate limit.</li>
<li>Taking this perspective, we show that the dynamical mean field theory is effectively second order, and relate this to the static mean field of singular learning theory.</li>
</ul>
<p>This post does get a little mathematically dense in places. We do try and make it somewhat more legible for those unfamiliar with mean field theory/many body physics though!</p>
<h2>Bonds between datapoints</h2>
<p>In some sense the starting point for this is the simplest object in machine learning: the loss function. For a model with parameters  and (for pedagogy) let's consider a finite dataset  with the  drawn i.i.d. from some data distribution , so the empirical loss becomes</p>
<p></p>
<p>with the population loss defined via the standard  limit.</p>
<p>One thing about the loss that sometimes goes underappreciated is that it looks a lot like what we might call a "parametrized Hamiltonian". To see this note that we can read the empirical loss as a total energy written as a sum of energies of individual "particles" in the system. Each datapoint is a particle, and  is its energy when the model has parameters .</p>
<p>Notice that every particle shares the same . To see why this is important imagine a black box with three lightbulbs on top who are wired in some unknown way, whose brightness reports the energies , , , and a panel of twenty unlabeled knobs. We know nothing about what the knobs do, but we want to understand how the lightbulbs are wired. Now imagine we start twisting the knobs and just seeing what they do. Knob 7 brightens A and B a little and dims C. Knob 12 brightens all three by different amounts. Knob 3 mostly does something to C. This is kind of messy, hard to interpret exactly what each knob does. So we start just kind of going nuts, twisting and turning the knobs in random ways and looking at the outputs. We sort of ignore exactly what knobs do what, and just kind of see how the lightbulbs change together. Looking at the bulb, one can see that maybe changes which change A are very likely to change B, maybe C always dims if both A and B brighten, etc. So we may not know exactly what each knob does, but we can kind of infer how strongly each bulb is wired to each other bulb</p>
<p>We can pretty simply put some math to this for the loss. Starting with the simple "two body" story, imagine we perturb  and for simplicity just take . The bond between two particles is the covariance of their energies under this perturbation:</p>
<p></p>
<p>A kind of nice result (that will become useful later) is that if we Taylor expand this then to first order,  with  which gives an approximation of the covariance like:</p>
<p></p>
<p>Averaging over random perturbations <em>sums out the parameter index</em> , leaving you with indices in the data space, so the parameters are exactly what gives the relationships between particles.</p>
<h3>Beyond pairwise bonds</h3>
<p>The above construction only captures <strong>two-body structure</strong>. It tells us whether two datapoints tend to respond together if we perturb parameter space. But there can be more complex relationships between several datapoints which cannot be reduced to simple pairwise bonds. For example, perhaps  and  are only weakly correlated, as are  and  and  and , but whenever the loss of  and  both increase, the energy of  almost always decreases. There is then some structure belonging to the triple  as a whole which is invisible if we only inspect the pairwise bonds.</p>
<p>This is the basic idea of a <strong>higher-order correlation</strong>. A three-point correlation asks how the energies of three particles vary together, a four-point correlation asks the same for four particles, and so on. There is an important subtlety, however. A raw three-point correlation can contain structure that is already explained by lower-order relationships. What we really want is the part of the three-particle relationship that is not captured by any of the lower order junk. This is captured by the <strong>joint cumulant</strong> (which we will talk about more later)</p>
<p></p>
<p>and similarly at higher orders. Cumulants therefore give us what in physics are often called <strong>connected</strong> or <strong>irreducible -body correlations</strong>.</p>
<p>The word <em>irreducible</em> is reflects the fact that we only want to capture only the parts that cannot be expressed by lower order terms. For example, at second order,</p>
<p></p>
<p>so the bond  is the irreducible two-body interaction. At third order,  measures whatever remains of the joint behavior of the three particles after the lower-order structure has been accounted for. At fourth order we ask what genuinely four-particle structure remains, and so on. Each order therefore answers roughly the question: <strong>what is new here that I could not have inferred from the orders below?</strong></p>
<p>In this sense, the data distribution define a whole hierarchy of interactions of different orders, all mediated through the same shared parameter vector . Two datapoints can be coupled because changing  changes both of their losses together, but collections of three, four, or more datapoints can also have collective responses which cannot be decomposed into pairwise effects.</p>
<p>Next, we consider what happens if instead of considering how  changes with respect to , we consider how  responds to changes in the population loss .</p>
<h2>The mean field of the data</h2>
<p>Moving from looking at bonds between individual particles to bonds between particles and some bulk structure made of many other particles is known in physics as a<em>mean field</em> approach. For those unfamiliar, the idea of a mean field approach is actually rather straightforward. Suppose we have a huge number of particles in a system. Taking a particle  and looking at how it interacts with every single other particle  can be difficult and unnecessary. What we then do is replace the infinite collection of individual particles by their average effect. If, say, all the particles are magnets, to know how  behaves from the electromagnetic interactions with all the other , we don't need to model the individual magnetic interactions. Instead we just pretend that all the other  are actually just a big field. This is why it is called a mean field, as we can replace complex interactions by creating a simple field to represent them.</p>
<h3>The generating function</h3>
<p>Since the mean field is generated by interactions, and these interactions are represented by cumulants, we start by defining the <em>joint cumulant generating function</em> of the particle energies under the prior,</p>
<p></p>
<p>Each  is like a weight that allows us to adjust the influence of a given datapoint. Setting every  gives the log partition function of the posterior, . So why is this called the cumulant generating function? Consider the first derivative, , which gives the mean energy of the particle.  The matrix of the second derivatives  gives the bond matrix, and defines the variance of the system. The third derivative defines the skewness, which is the three body interactions. So the th cumulant defines the  body interactions. The general notation for the th cumulant tends to be .</p>
<p>The cumulant generating function we just wrote out seems made of individual data interactions. However, we can actually decompose this into a "bulk" part and an individual part. We show explicitly how this works in the next section.</p>
<h3>The second-order mean field</h3>
<p>The general trick in a mean field theory, as discussed, is to replace particle-particle interactions with particle-bulk interactions. In our case, the "bulk" is the population loss. How do we then move from the interactions between per-sample losses to the interactions between the bulk and the samples? To show this we are going to start with the lowest order data-data interactions. First, we split each particle's energy into a population part and its own deviation:</p>
<p></p>
<p>Tildes always mean <em>centered over the population</em>, so  has mean zero under  for every . The total energy becomes</p>
<p></p>
<p>Next we simply define 's coupling to the bulk as</p>
<p></p>
<p>We can now build out our mean field. To do this, substitute  into both slots of  and expand. Covariance is bilinear, so two sums of two terms give four pieces describing interactions:</p>
<p>
</p>
<p>The first three terms all pass through the bulk. That's the same move as in the magnet picture: particle  doesn't interact with  directly but with the field the bulk produces, and  does the same. To write those three terms more compactly, expand the coupling to the bulk the same way:</p>
<p></p>
<p>So  already contains two of our four pieces: the bulk with itself, and  with the bulk. Likewise  contains the bulk with itself and the bulk with . Adding  therefore picks up all three bulk-mediated terms, but it counts  twice. To avoid overcounting we subtract the overlap once and get:</p>
<p></p>
<p>The mean-field bond  is built entirely from each particle's coupling to the bulk, so it needs only one number per particle rather than one per pair. The residual  is whatever two particles share that does not go through the bulk, and it is what one would call the "two-body correction."</p>
<p>If we think about this, it tells us that  two datapoints can look correlated for two very different reasons. There might be some general set of structures in the model (like induction heads) that broadly lower the population loss because a lot of samples get a slightly lower loss by exploiting this mechanism. This looks like bulk coupling. The other possibility is two samples might rely on much more specific, targeted structures like the knowledge of the birthdays of all the royal family. These look much less like bulk coupling as probably not much of the bulk actually needs this information to perform well.</p>
<h3>Higher order mean field from the generating function</h3>
<p>The bond decomposition was the bulk-particle case of something that holds at every order, meaning that we can decompose interactions into bulk interactions with  interacting particles/datapoints. The math here looks a bit messy, but it really is just the higher order version of the previous section.</p>
<p>Start by writing  as its cumulant expansion around :</p>
<p></p>
<p>Here the cumulants are taken under the prior . Now substitute  into every slot. Joint cumulants are multilinear, so an th-order cumulant splits into  pieces, one for each way of filling each slot with either  or a . For , these are exactly the four routes from before: bulk with bulk,  with bulk, bulk with , and  with .</p>
<p>To organize the pieces, sort them by , the number of slots that carry a particle's own identity. If  of the  slots are individual, there are  ways to choose which ones. Each of the remaining  bulk slots contributes , since  doesn't care which index it came with. Using , this gives</p>
<p></p>
<p>Every term in this sum is one kind of interaction, and the three pieces of the decomposition are just three ranges of . This is all pretty nasty but we can actually put some meaning to what these orders actually are.</p>
<p><strong>The  terms</strong> have no individual sample slows slots, so they are pure bulk with bulk at every order, , which we can then clump into its own cumulant expansion like:</p>
<p></p>
<p>This piece depends on the weights of individual samples only through their total . It cannot tell which individual particles are present, only how much weight the bulk carries.</p>
<p><strong>The  terms</strong> have no bulk slots. They are the direct correlations between private deviations at every order, and they also get their own cumulant expansion:</p>
<p></p>
<p><strong>The  terms</strong> form . These are interactions in which particles and the bulk both take part. The  terms, , describe a single particle coupling to the bulk. They are the all-orders version of the  piece inside  from earlier. Terms with  describe several particles interacting while the bulk also participates, so some genuine many-body structure lives here too. In physics we would say it is "dressed by the bulk". So now we can write the whole cumulant generating function like:</p>
<p></p>
<p></p>
<p>Now here is a cool trick. The cross terms can be absorbed exactly by letting the bulk set the background measure, and the remaining fluctuations of individuals are then measured on top of it:</p>
<p></p>
<p>This decomposition looks like it's doing something clever, but it's actually just a split of the reweighting. Since every particle's energy is the population loss plus its own deviation, , the total reweighting splits cleanly:</p>
<p></p>
<p>The first piece only knows how much total weight  you've put on the data, and it reweights the prior the same way no matter which datapoints carry that weight. The second piece is where individual identities live: each datapoint's weird little quirks, weighted by its own . Because the exponential turns this sum into a product, we can do the reweighting in two stages where we first let the bulk reshape the prior into , and then average the individuals over <em>that</em> reshaped measure rather than the original prior.</p>
<p>Seen this way, the two terms answer two different questions.  asks: <em>if the data were a featureless bulk with total weight , where would the parameters end up under the Bayesian posterior?</em> The second term asks: <em>given that the bulk has already put the parameters there, how much do the specific datapoints you actually have, with their individual weirdness, still move things around?</em> This actually shows up in <a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/algebraic-geometry-and-statistical-learning-theory/9C8FD1BDC817E2FC79117C7F41544A3A">singular learning theory</a>.</p>
<h3>The singular learning theory relationship</h3>
<p>The cross terms are precisely what converts connected cumulants measured under the prior into connected cumulants measured under the mean-field measure . Now taking , that measure is the population posterior normally studied in SLT. The free energy therefore splits exactly into a mean-field part and a fluctuation part:</p>
<p></p>
<p>SLT then tells us that the mean-field term obeys Watanabe's free energy asymptotics,</p>
<p></p>
<p>where  is the learning coefficient (RLCT) and  its multiplicity. The classical result of SLT is asymptotically that</p>
<p></p>
<p>As . We can interpret this as saying that the bulk determines the leading asymptotics, and the identities of individual particles enter only at . So, SLT is something like a theory of the bulk.</p>
<h2>The dynamical mean field of the data</h2>
<p>So far the mean-field construction has been static. We replaced the collection of individual data particles by the average field generated by the population. Learning, however, is a path through parameter space. That is, we we have a path through parameter space  then our mean field construction must evolve in time. This sounds like a dynamical mean field theory. So how do we go from the static mean field to the dynamical one?</p>
<p>Before giving some of the more mathy bits, the core construction admits a nice intuitive description. The first thing to do is imagine we have a fixed starting point , a data distribution , and some fixed number of steps  we are going to run SGD for, and take the learning rate to be sufficiently small. To start off, suppose we run SGD with a batch size of 1 for  steps, and we look at the distribution of all the paths it took during that time. We then do the same thing for batch size 2, then 3, and so on, taking . As this happens, the distribution of trajectories starts to asymptotically concentrate around the deterministic gradient descent path.</p>
<p>To see why this is a mean field theory, think of each datapoint's gradient as a force: a push on the parameters in the direction that would lower that datapoint's loss. Just as with the loss, we can split each push into the bulk's part, the population gradient, and the datapoint's individual part, its deviation from the bulk. Every SGD step is then the bulk's push plus the average of the private pushes of whichever  datapoints happened to be drawn. The thing then is that the interactions between each datapoint on the loss level is evolving, and that evolution is actually governed by how their gradients interact. For example, if two different samples both give the same gradient direction, following the gradient of one basically must pull the other one along, meaning their evolution is coupled.</p>
<p>Below we formalize this and show that it is effectively what gives the standard stochastic differential equation description of SGD. For those familiar with DMFT we give the basic outline of the two-time force covariance and the response kernel.</p>
<h3>From a cloud of SGD paths to one mean-field path</h3>
<p>We start now with the gradient of the loss</p>
<p></p>
<p>which is the force exerted by a data particle  on itself/others, and suppose datapoints are drawn from a population distribution . The population "force" is the gradient of the population loss</p>
<p></p>
<p>Now at SGD step , draw a minibatch</p>
<p></p>
<p>and define the stochastic gradient</p>
<p></p>
<p>The update is then</p>
<p></p>
<p>A particular sequence of batches therefore determines an entire trajectory through parameter space. With , each update is determined by a single particle, so there is a large cloud of possible trajectories. With , each step averages two forces and the cloud becomes narrower. With  it narrows further. As  becomes large, the stochastic gradient concentrates around the population gradient and the bundle of SGD trajectories collapses toward a single mean path.</p>
<p>We now use the same sort of trick we used earlier to rewrite the gradient as the population average plus noise:</p>
<p></p>
<p>where</p>
<p></p>
<p>Substituting this into the minibatch gradient gives</p>
<p></p>
<p>It is useful to define</p>
<p></p>
<p>so that</p>
<p></p>
<p>The stochastic gradient has therefore been split into a population mean field and a fluctuation around it. This is actually the standard gradient noise description of SGD.</p>
<p>Now by construction,</p>
<p></p>
<p>Because the particles in the batch are independent,</p>
<p></p>
<p>and hence</p>
<p></p>
<p>For sufficiently large , the multivariate central limit theorem gives</p>
<p></p>
<p>so we may write approximately</p>
<p></p>
<p>where</p>
<p></p>
<p>This tells us how larger batches suppress the fluctuations. Specifically, it says that averaging over  datapoints suppresses the force fluctuations like , or equivalently suppresses their variance like .</p>
<p>Notice that the stochastic force is actually generated precisely by <strong>pairwise disagreement between data particles</strong>. If  and  are independent draws from , then</p>
<p></p>
<p>If almost every particle tries to move the parameters in the same direction, then replacing one sampled particle by another barely changes the update and the trajectory cloud is narrow. If different particles pull strongly in different directions, then which particles happen to enter a batch matters and the cloud becomes broad. At small batch sizes, any change can be very broad. So, for large enough batches the fluctuations around the average path are generated by two body data-data interactions. Next, we are going to write this as a DMFT.</p>
<h3>Writing down the DMFT</h3>
<p>So, what we would generally do here is move to the SGD SDE limit and work in that for formal reasons. But, for the sake of this blog post it is sufficient to just write it out as generic weakly fluctuating dynamics:</p>
<p></p>
<p>where  for finite-batch fluctuations (where we drop the learning rate for simplicity) and just assume we have absorbed a very small learning rate into the gradient and noise. The choice of  is because this is essentially like a semiclassical limit. The sign of  is conventional and will not matter for its covariance. Moving to the SGD SDE is formally straightforward if one desires.</p>
<p>As , the path approaches . We then write the expansion around the path as</p>
<p></p>
<p>Here, we use a bit of a trick which is actually important for downstream theory, but whose formal impact we will leave for later work. Expanding the dynamics to first order gives</p>
<p></p>
<p>where</p>
<p></p>
<p>We can now define the <em>response kernel</em>  by</p>
<p></p>
<p>Then</p>
<p></p>
<p>A force fluctuation applied at time  is therefore propagated to time  by . If</p>
<p></p>
<p>then the two-time covariance of the path fluctuations is</p>
<p></p>
<p>For the data field,</p>
<p></p>
<p>so the same data particle can produce correlated forces at widely separated points on the path. This is one of the reasons why the DMFT perspective is cool. It kind of gives the natural construction of influence functions over time.</p>
<p>For those familiar with DMFT, a more fully self-consistent DMFT-like theory appears at the next orders, when the fluctuations modify the statistics of the force and generate memory or self-energy kernels. But we have found that the leading order theory is actually sufficiently rich for our purposes. Also, there is a relationship with the NTK literature, and the neural tangent hierarchy, but, since this is meant to be a simple blog post we are going to leave that for some future work.</p>
<h3>Quadratic dynamics with a non-quadratic loss</h3>
<p>One way to interpret this is that  under the usual finite-moment and independence conditions on the data is that the Gaussian noise-Hessian closure description is actually very good theory for certain parts of training. The (anistropic) Gaussian noise tends to hold across all of training with the exact distribution changing over time. Now, while this is not what this post is about, the local quadratic contribution of the dynamics can be thought of as describing the movement of training during a "phase". The is, the local dynamics admit such a description apart from isolated times during training which look like a phase transition. This is the subject of an upcoming paper, and is one of the nice things you get out of the DMFT perspective</p>
<p>It is important to note that the statement is <strong>not</strong> that the loss itself is always well approximated by a quadratic function of the parameters. It is that the <em>collective force fluctuations around the mean trajectory</em> become Gaussian, and hence quadratic at leading order, because they are produced by averaging many data particles, and that at standard points during training the leading order term of the deterministic dynamics dominates. This gives what one would call a second order effective theory of the dynamics, which can be explored and manipulated.</p>
<h2>Conclusion</h2>
<p>The above constructions present the basic framework underlying some of my personal research directions as of late. Whether or not they are useful for anyone else remains to be seen but if you have made it to this point, thanks for reading!</p>
