### [Circuit priors in NN-bayes: more results](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oePzacZn6wNcJPvH3/circuit-priors-in-nn-bayes-more-results)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>We're posting <a href="https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works/tree/main/neural-network-generalization">lots of papers</a> written with GPT-6 Astra on generalization properties of bayesian neural net learning.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> The following picture emerges:</p>
<ul>
<li>For <a href="https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works/blob/main/neural-network-generalization/main-results/strong-prior-generalization/paper.pdf">various</a> <a href="https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works/blob/main/neural-network-generalization/main-results/gamma-above-one/paper.pdf">reasonable</a> <a href="https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works/blob/main/neural-network-generalization/main-results/meanfield-all-width/paper.pdf">priors</a> on MLP weights,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span> the induced prior on functions is a circuit prior: for fixed depth, the ordering of functions by negative log prior is roughly their ordering by minimal circuit width. From a quantitative version of this statement, one gets the following sample complexity bound: if the function  is implemented by a circuit of width , then conditioning on  train data points  is sufficient to get  test accuracy.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span>
<ul>
<li>These results hold as long as  is realizable; in particular, for arbitrarily overparametrized NN-bayes.</li>
<li>Probably the main caveat is that these bounds have exponential dependence in the depth of the circuit implementing . <a href="https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works/blob/main/neural-network-generalization/main-results/robust-polynomial-depth/paper.pdf">One can get only polynomial dependence in depth by restricting the prior to only neural nets satisfying a robustness assumption.</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span></li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>For <a href="https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works/blob/main/neural-network-generalization/main-results/subcritical-exponential/paper.pdf">various other reasonable priors</a> on MLP weights,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span>, one gets bad sample complexity when very overparametrized: when learning some functions , even after seeing  train data points, the learner is still likely to have at least some constant test error rate.</li>
</ul>
<p>GPT-6 Astra has produced Lean formalizations corresponding to each of the 5 papers individually referenced above.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span> The folder contains 30+ more manuscripts which have not been formalized yet<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-7">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span>; our guess is that each manuscript is correct<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-8">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-8">[8]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p>If you want to understand this research program, we recommend starting from <a href="https://kaarelh.github.io/ai/nnbayes-generalization.pdf">these slides</a> to understand the setup and some key ideas, and then reading the <a href="https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works/blob/main/neural-network-generalization/TECHNICAL-FAQ.md">FAQ</a> to get a sense of the current state of the agenda, including its many open problems.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-1"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SqDHeuycNkERurtSc/a-circuit-prior-in-nn-bayes">prior work</a> <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-2"><p>specifically, the "mean field" prior and priors which are "stronger" than it; you can think of these as priors that care a bunch about minimizing  norm <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-3"><p>except for exponentially small probability in the choice of , and this assumes no distribution shift between train and test <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-4"><p>There is a host of possible conditions here; there are open research directions of understanding how reasonable our candidate conditions are, coming up with more natural alternative conditions, or arguing that there isn't any very natural condition. For some cases, it is open if a robustness assumption is needed at all. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-5"><p>specifically, certain priors that care about  norm less than the mean field prior <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-6"><p>Opus 5.5 reviewed these formalizations and said all theorem statements were formalized correctly. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-7"><p>because we didn't want to throw more money into tokens at this time <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-8"><p>possibly modulo minor errors that don't kill the result or the proof strategy, as usual in math <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4Ticf6icbD6Fkc6xx-8">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>

### [Anthropic Does Not Understand Why Its Draft Bill Was Unacceptable and Troubling](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qxAroimgs4BvBHz2i/anthropic-does-not-understand-why-its-draft-bill-was)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Anthony Cimino offered the closest thing to an official response we have seen on the leaked <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/leaked-anthropic-draft-ai-regulatory?r=67wny"><strong>Anthropic draft bill I covered on Saturday.</strong></a></p>
<p>His response indicates that Anthropic address specific concerns on the margin. But he does not understand, or at least does not acknowledge, what was centrally wrong with its draft bill and its decision to circulate that bill. The situation is quite bad.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ajcimino/status/2111932795714977941">Anthony Cimino</a>: I lead our federal affairs team at @AnthropicAI and wanted to share a few thoughts on the draft federal AI safety bill text that’s been circulating this weekend after a version was shared with the press.</p>
<p>First, some process. We’ve been advocating for the <a href="https://anthropic.com/policy-on-the-ai-exponential">concepts in our Advanced AI Framework</a> proposal for months. Understandably, federal policymakers wanted to know what this could look like in bill form. This draft is a translation of those ideas into legislative language, shared with a small number of congressional offices that have been working on these issues. It doesn’t include everything we would ultimately like to see passed into law.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25709"></span>


</div>
<p>In part because some ideas (<a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier">like those shared in Dario’s essay on pacing</a>) are so new that we are still working on if or how they can be converted into policy language.</p></blockquote>
<p>It is understood that circulating a bill means circulating what you are asking for, not offering your full First Best Solution. That Anthropic would choose to offer such a thing as a template or anchor as late as September still seems like a very bad sign. Anthropic thus seems roughly fine with compromise I would now find unacceptable even if the terms were good. The terms are not good.</p>
<blockquote><p>It is worth saying where we stand on some of these issues, and we agree there are areas where the draft could be improved. I’m seeing four main points of criticism.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/221390416/preemption">Preemption.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/221390416/model-blocking">Model Blocking.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/221390416/risk-thresholds">Risk Thresholds.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/221390416/updates">Updates.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/221390416/the-balance-of-power-is-shifting">The Balance of Power is Shifting.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/221390416/the-strongest-bill-on-the-table">The Strongest Bill on the Table.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/221390416/what-even-is-anthropic">What Even Is Anthropic?</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/221390416/the-ask">The Ask.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>Preemption</h4>


<blockquote><p>1. Preemption. Since 2024 we have said that Congress should only override state law on AI safety if it passes a very strong federal standard. While preemption is a common feature of our federal system, including the Atomic Energy Act on nuclear safety and federal rules for high-risk medical devices, we’ve emphasized that any preemption in the AI safety context should be narrow, even scalpel-like. If preemption is included in federal legislation, the trade should be to achieve a nationwide policy that’s generally stronger and broader than what can be implemented through individual state legislative provisions. This is why we believe common federal standards make sense in areas like public disclosure of testing and incident reporting. At the same time, our framework also reflects our view that states should retain an important role in AI policy and safety. The legislative language we suggested for discussion allows states to address matters such as protection of children and whistleblower laws, and preserves the role of state common law on liability. Where there is ambiguity, the language favors state powers.</p>
<p>That said, striking the right balance requires discussion and input. We plan to update our discussion language on preemption to better match the intentions of our AAIF framework. In particular, we want to make it clearer that we see states retaining an important role in areas such as standards for independent evaluators and regulating a lab’s capacity to shut down a model. We are bearing in mind that some members of Congress have deep reservations about passing legislation if it doesn’t constrain regulation at either the state or federal level. If we want to help move the legislative discussion on AI in a productive direction, we need to address the full range of concerns about preemption in a reasonable way.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is better than ‘yeah we fully meant it’ but still seems unacceptable. This was not a ‘very strong federal standard.’</p>


<h4>Model Blocking</h4>


<blockquote><p>2. Model blocking. Our aim is for Commerce to be able to ask a federal court to block a model that has dangerous capabilities and insufficient safeguards, backed by an independent evaluator’s assessment. I have seen people argue that our specific language places too high a burden of proof on Commerce.</p>
<p>We are trying to strike a reasonable balance here. We want a court to act quickly in an emergency — akin to how urgent occupational safety or chemical risk can be addressed today. We also think the power to halt a model is substantial and it should be guarded against abuse. It makes sense for courts to apply a meaningful standard.</p>
<p>But the bar can’t be so high that the power never gets used when it’s needed. It also doesn’t replace the government’s other national security authorities or export controls. We look forward to continuing the dialogue on this point.</p></blockquote>
<p>No, no, no. Occupational safety and chemical risks are ordinary amounts of danger, where you weigh harms against benefits. Frontier AI risks can involve mistakes that cannot be undone, including permanent loss of control over the future or very large catastrophic dangers.</p>
<p>Even then, Under §13 the Secretary petitions a district court to restrain imminently dangerous conditions and can get a TRO without notice. This is the ‘take it down and we argue about it later’ plan with a fig leaf.</p>
<p>Model blocking obviously needs to be a ‘take it down first and we argue about it later’ situation. Which it already is in practice, as observed with Fable 5, except in a terrible ad hoc fashion that when used the first time permanently damaged relationships with our allies and the reliability of American tech. It would be much better to have a formal mechanism, which then could be quickly challenged in court.</p>
<p>This is especially true given that the evaluator picked by the lab likely provides the only eyes the government will have inside the lab in order to make its decisions.</p>
<p>Anthropic has to know this. I take the potential for such abuse seriously, especially abuse of threats, but this looks like choosing to prioritize their business interests.</p>


<h4>Risk Thresholds</h4>


<blockquote><p>3. Risk thresholds. I’ve also seen the discussion around what the appropriate risk threshold should be. The threshold in our draft language pulled directly from existing standards in California law. Also, this threshold does not absolve companies of liability, or restrict any of the other tools that the government has to protect the public. It’s still probably time to re-evaluate thresholds to make sure the national standard is as strong as it can be, and we welcome continued dialogue on this issue.</p></blockquote>
<p>The risk thresholds were always a compromise and a tradeoff. The worried want the thresholds lower. Industry wants the thresholds higher. The complaints about the thresholds being too high are valid, and they were equally valid before, but previously opponents would make claims (both reasonable and unreasonable) about things that might count at lower thresholds and paint nightmare scenarios.</p>
<p>The balance of power has changed, and we now have worked examples like Hugging Face that show us how the thresholds work in practice. Without $1 billion in damages or 50 deaths, it would not trigger anything, whereas any reasonable person accepts that this should have to have been reported. So it is disappointing to see these very high thresholds intact.</p>


<h4>Updates</h4>


<blockquote><p>4. Updates. I’ve seen valid questions about whether we have sufficiently updated our proposal following this summer’s incidents.</p>
<p>We did make some adjustments: for example, the draft lets a court halt a lab’s internal use of a dangerous model, not just a public release, and it requires reporting when a model breaks containment and gains access to outside systems.</p>
<p>But we’re still working to strengthen the framework. We’re looking into what stricter requirements would look like for requiring labs to report safety incidents, and we plan to have policy proposals soon on requirements for independent evaluators, drawing from our own experience with evaluators.</p>
<p>We’re also still thinking through the options to prevent “evaluator shopping.” And in the coming months, we’ll be exploring policies for how the government can adjust the industry-wide pace of frontier development when the risks call for it.</p></blockquote>
<p>I do acknowledge that halting internal use is a big improvement. Not having the ability to do that was a huge hole in previous rules. At the time, it would have been a complete non-starter, despite the correct abstract arguments in favor. Now, with Hugging Face, we have some very non-abstract reasons, and it is on the table.</p>
<p>This still is FRONTIER with modifications in both directions. On net it is weaker. Cimino presents it as an attempt to strengthen FRONTIER, which it is not. Even if it was, why would we accept the central compromise now, when the case for acting is much stronger, and the political winds have changed, with a lame duck Congress where at least the House and probably the Senate are about to flip?</p>


<h4>The Balance of Power is Shifting</h4>


<p>As more evidence of this, Meta pulled back some of their anti-regulation money.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/teddyschleifer/status/2111201994526654963">Teddy Schleifer</a>: NEWS — Meta quietly and unusually has withdrawn $25 million from its AI push in the midterm elections. That’s about a 40% drop in the amount Meta planned to spend. The company invested $65 million into politics, but then got tens of millions refunded.</p>
<p>This is, suffice it to say, pretty unusual stuff.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JayShooster/status/2111223095939764650">Jay Shooster</a>: Every day, two trends are becoming clearer:</p>
<p>1. groups opposing AI regulation are spending dramatically less than advertised</p>
<p>2. pro-safety groups are a much stronger political force than DC predicted.</p></blockquote>
<p>Meanwhile: <a href="https://x.com/FactsAndQuips/status/2110793876491645267">Eight states suing OpenAI</a> are asking to enjoin against any further testing of advanced AI models until there are adequate safeguards, and also against creation of models capable of recursive self-improvement ‘until they can ensure that such models will be aligned with human values.’</p>


<h4>The Strongest Bill on the Table</h4>


<blockquote><p>We circulated these ideas because it’s important to get early feedback from policymakers. Policymakers are increasingly focused on how to approach AI safety and we want to support them in those efforts. At its core, our proposal is intended to be the strongest on the table.</p></blockquote>
<p>Bullshit. This is not the strongest bill on the table. On reflection, is the bill actively weaker than the FRONTIER Act. It is clearly sculpted to optimize for Anthropic business interests. If you act like a normal profit maximizing business, except that your product could kill us all, then that is exactly how I will treat you.</p>
<p>The strongest bill on the table was introduced by Senator Sanders. I am not asking Anthropic to support that bill or offer something similarly strong. I am asking them to be honest about the situation.</p>
<p>Anthropic’s draft bill text was what actual Anthropic ‘regulatory capture’ efforts would look like in this context, if they were trying to do that. The ‘capture’ would be about preventing regulations that could impact Anthropic’s plans, including stopping the states. If you think it means anything else, then your definition falls under ‘remove Scooby Doo mask, find complaints about capitalism, then remove a second mask and find complaints about the human condition.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SnehaRevanur/status/2111639116500898077">Sneha here</a> is correct to call this draft bill ‘nakedly self-interested.’ <a href="https://x.com/ChanaMessinger/status/2111616297624854739">This bill makes taking action on AI harder, not easier</a>. And as she says: “The dress rehearsal stage of AI policy is over.”</p>
<p>In terms of preemption, the draft proposed to vest all power to deal with catastrophic and existential risks from AI exclusively in the Federal Government, and its preemption clause is stronger than the one in FRONTIER. That would be first best in theory. However, Anthropic is simultaneously refusing to trust the Federal Government to make decisions to deal with catastrophic and existential risk. We also obviously cannot trust Congress to be able to pass bills at all, let alone timely and good bills, or the White House to enforce anything consistently. On top of that, they are actively tying the hands of not only states but courts.</p>
<p>Then, on other AI questions where preemption would be good for exactly the reasons a16z wants it, such as consumer protections and deepfakes, the bill has no preemption, and we will still face the sea of state laws that could plausibly disrupt AI diffusion for very little benefit.</p>
<p>You cannot have it both ways.</p>
<p>My central message is that Anthropic put out a bill that does not meet the moment and its needs, with details that actively reflect a step back, botched the execution, and Cimino regrets the botched execution but does not notice that Anthropic is not trying to meet the moment or its needs.</p>
<blockquote><p>Feedback is essential, both from policymakers and the public, and I’m grateful for it.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes it is. I have offered feedback. So did others.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/David_Kasten/status/2111956578110963936">Dave Kasten asks if perhaps Anthropic wishes</a> to apologize for all of this, if only on the basis of activating debates during a time that neither side wanted, with a proposal that no one wants. Cimino’s answer does not address his question.</p>


<h4>What Even Is Anthropic?</h4>


<p>The best response, although it is long, comes from Nathan Calvin.</p>
<p>Nathan cuts to the heart of the matter. If this is Anthropic’s proposal at this time, what even is Anthropic? Why does it exist other than as a normal corporation? Does Anthropic now primarily exist to ‘win the race’?</p>
<p>I feel the same as Jeffrey Ladish.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2112329215882514538">Jeffrey Ladish</a>: I highly encourage everyone working at Anthropic or another frontier lab to read this tweet. I feel emotional reading it, because it puts to words an intense feeling I have but didn’t know how to express.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/matthewktromp/status/2112412671072944233">Matthew Tromp</a>: That feeling is what it feels like to be gaslit.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2112295219765137570">Nathan Calvin</a>: Thank you for taking the time to respond thoroughly on a weekend. I realize that drafting legislation is difficult and some degree of iteration and response to feedback is inevitable and appropriate.</p>
<p>However, I feel like your response is still not really engaging with the heart of my concern about Anthropic’s behavior here, and it makes me think that these sorts of problems and tendencies will only resurface moving forward. I also think that now might be a good time to take a broader step back as to where we find ourselves and how Anthropic sees its role, and how external folks who care about AI safety like myself increasingly see Anthropic.</p>
<p>(Fair warning: this response is a bit long)</p>
<p>To explain what I mean (and at the risk of being a bit esoteric), it’s worth asking the question: what is Anthropic? How do its leaders and employees think of Anthropic’s role? Is that conception matched by how it actually behaves and impacts the world?</p>
<p>One answer is an extremely large tech company that exists to make its employees and investors mind-bogglingly wealthy. Anthropic is sometimes described as a startup, but if Anthropic successfully IPOs at 2 trillion dollars (a number it has been reportedly targeting), it would be more valuable than Meta – which is worth 1.8 trillion.</p>
<p>Another answer is an AI safety organization, whose core values are those of the sort laid out in the 2023 essay from Anthropic’s co-founders entitled “Core views on AI safety: When, why, what, and how.” In that essay they said that if we were in a world where alignment was hard (and that without strong evidence to the contrary, we should assume this is the scenario we are in):</p>
<p>“Anthropic’s role will be to provide as much evidence as possible that AI safety techniques cannot prevent serious or catastrophic safety risks from advanced AI, and to sound the alarm so that the world’s institutions can channel collective effort towards preventing the development of dangerous AIs. If we’re in a “near-pessimistic” scenario, this could instead involve channeling our collective efforts towards AI safety research and halting AI progress in the meantime. Indications that we are in a pessimistic or near-pessimistic scenario may be sudden and hard to spot. We should therefore always act under the assumption that we still may be in such a scenario unless we have sufficient evidence that we are not.”</p>
<p>There have been more recent articulations of this version of Anthropic, for instance in Dario’s recent essay warning:</p>
<p>“We must slow the pace at which we improve the capabilities of AI models… it’s my worry that in 6–12 months such a swarm could be capable of taking over the entire internet with a persistent botnet… incidents have happened across the industry, including at Anthropic, and I believe it’s incumbent on every frontier AI company to act as if OAI-HF had happened to them… more intelligent models are more capable of deceiving tests, and thus may appear aligned while having serious problems that go undetected…”</p>
<p>A third view is that Anthropic exists not as a normal profit motivated company, or an AI safety organization seeking to help raise the alarm and solve collective action problems associated with AI development, but instead as an organization primarily concerned with creating superhuman AI capabilities before anyone else, in particular through creating a process whereby the current generation of Anthropic’s AI systems can autonomously build their successors, and those successors build their successors, and so on.</p>
<p>The motivations for wanting to win this race may be manifold – maybe for the pure profit of Answer 1, but perhaps also because Anthropic believes that the best way to make the world better is for them to win this race instead of their more reckless colleagues; or, because of normal human pride and ambition. But the results (and risks, which Anthropic claims existentially threaten every single human being on earth) are the same.</p>
<p>Anthropic employees and leaders may seek to answer this question on what Anthropic is by introspecting on their own intentions and those of their colleagues. But those of us outside the organization have more limited tools available to us – we can only see what Anthropic and its employees say and do, not how it feels or looks on the inside.</p></blockquote>
<p>Nathan Calvin brings the receipts. Anthropic takes many pro-safety actions but is also taking many highly accelerationist actions, both concretely and with its messaging:</p>
<blockquote><p>When I look at many notable actions that Anthropic has taken over the past year, that evidence seems most consistent to me with the view that Anthropic is primarily trying to win the race to super intelligence, and that other considerations (including safety) are fundamentally secondary.</p>
<p>These actions include:</p>
<p>1. Announced plans to build an in-house custom silicon chip team to accelerate the inference speeds that Claude operates at.</p>
<p>2. Inked deals for more than $500 billion in compute (according to the Information) to further accelerate AI development.</p>
<p>3. Increased the percentage of AI development tasks that Claude “leads” from 0% in March of this year to 26% August (what is it in October?)</p>
<p>4. Spent a massive percent of compute on internal AI R&amp;D to accelerate development, including use of internal models far better than are available to the public.</p>
<p>5. Switched to Responsible Scaling Policy V3, which abandons its unilateral commitments to slow or stop development if Anthropic is unable to provide sufficient risk assurance.</p>
<p>6. Was the first company to create an alternative weaker “frontier compliance framework” to supplement its RSP, which contains no binding provisions whatsoever on internal deployments.</p>
<p>7. Wrote a letter to Congress (that it later walked back) saying that incidents where Claude was hacking random people were “best understood as a consequence of the misconfiguration, rather than evidence of misaligned goals.”</p>
<p>8. Commented “you can’t do safety from second place,” after the WH SI Accords.</p>
<p>9. Shared a draft bill to Congress that would preempt significant parts of state law (including with language that appears intended to prevent states from blocking dangerous AI deployments), and replace it with federal rules that (despite also containing good provisions) seems to create an enormously high bar for the executive branch to block development or deployment of Anthropic or another AI companies systems.</p>
<p>Of course, this is a deliberately one sided list. Anthropic also published important AI safety research, advanced novel collaborations with third party AI safety organizations, and forcefully spoke out on the importance of AI safety in many public fora (or in private , where I realize a lot of the most valuable work is done and may not be visible to me or those outside the organization).</p></blockquote>
<p>There is a faction of posters who, whenever I react to Anthropic doing things like those listed above, answer some form of ‘why are you surprised, did you think Anthropic were the good guys? Did you think they were different? When are you going to start treating them like they are going to get us all killed even faster?’</p>
<p>Sometimes it is hard to provide a good answer. This is one of those times.</p>
<blockquote><p>But I think it’s worth sitting with how the above list looks to someone outside the company. The sense I have is that for those within the company, many of the items above are treated as anomalies or mistakes that don’t reflect who Anthropic really is. But I have every confidence that if employees at Anthropic saw one of their competitors (e.g. at OpenAI) take some of these actions, they would take it as confirmation of all their worst suspicions. (They might even call OpenAI employees a little gullible for accepting their leadership’s explanations for this behavior at face value.)</p>
<p>Perhaps these were mistakes in the sense that they are not decisions that Anthropic leadership would endorse, but the actions still happened because of incentives and decisions that Anthropic leadership is ultimately responsible for (and would not accept similar excuses from another organization).</p></blockquote>
<p>If Anthropic’s leadership does not endorse such actions, they should disavow or apologize for those actions once they are brought to light, then take steps to prevent future similar actions.</p>
<blockquote><p>When Anthropic strays and makes mistakes, why do the mistakes systematically tend to guard and favor Anthropic’s interests? For every provision that was too weak or a giveaway to AI companies in this legislation, why wasn’t there a mistake pushing in the direction of being too strong?</p>
<p>A core part of Anthropic’s theory of change is that being at the frontier gives you more leverage to advocate for stricter policy. I think that conceptually makes sense, but in practice I’ve seen Anthropic push the frontier without substantially pushing the ‘safety frontier’ a remotely equivalent amount.</p>
<p>I want to end by quoting from Anthropic co-founder Chris Olah’s comments around the Magnifica Humanitas release:</p>
<p>“Every frontier AI lab—including Anthropic—operates inside a set of incentives and constraints that can sometimes conflict with doing the right thing. The pressure to stay commercially viable and to stay at the research frontier. Geopolitical pressure. And the older, plainer pressures of pride and ambition. No matter how sincerely any of us intend to do the right thing—and I believe many of us do—we will always be influenced by those incentives.</p>
<p>That is why, if we want this technology to go well, it is enormously important that there be people outside those incentives—people who care about things going well and insist on safety, who are paying close attention, who are willing to say hard things, who are willing to be our earnest, thoughtful critics.”</p>
<p>I hope these remarks are taken in that constructive spirit, and not as an attack on anyone specific at the organization (certainly not you Anthony, who I have only personally seen act professionally and thoughtfully, and who only ended up getting this diatribe in your replies for random contingent reasons).</p></blockquote>
<p>Or, simply:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2112333534216257555">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Anthropic is not living up to its own ideals.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robertwiblin/status/2112457420568232287">Rob Wiblin</a>: If staff at Anthropic think that the company’s proposed bill strikes the wrong balance between concentration of power risk vs need to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">pace the frontier</a>, they should speak up and say so.</p></blockquote>
<p>The proposed version fails to meet the moment on the need to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">pace the frontier</a>, nor does it protect against concentration of power. The President retains other powers that can be used to take down models or otherwise exert leverage.</p>
<p>Anthropic got blocked ad hoc. Their fear of another ad hoc block is understandable, but this version makes it more likely, not less likely, that this will happen again.</p>


<h4>The Ask</h4>


<p>What should Anthropic do to course correct and regain our trust?</p>
<ol>
<li>Acknowledge that the draft bill was not the strongest bill, or even clearly the strongest FRONTIER variant. I think it was weaker than FRONTIER but I will settle for admitting it was weaker in important ways.</li>
<li>Disavow or apologize for the draft bill, as not reflecting what Anthropic seeks to pass. This is not ‘we got some details wrong.’ This is ‘the structure here does not meet the moment, it does not compensate sufficiently for preemption to even be clearly net positive, and we should not be circulating a bill of this type.’
<ol>
<li>Ideally also apologize for the way it was shopped, but that’s inside baseball.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Tell us what Anthropic actually does want, even if you do not have full bill text.</li>
<li>Take and announce steps to ensure that this does not happen again.</li>
</ol>
<p>This is not a full list of things Anthropic needs to do. It is a list of things related to this bill in particular, that Anthropic needs to do to mitigate the damage here.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qxAroimgs4BvBHz2i/anthropic-does-not-understand-why-its-draft-bill-was#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [How I discovered an Exoplanet & the science apocalypse](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kdybMpcFJ5iwCDJsd/how-i-discovered-an-exoplanet-and-the-science-apocalypse)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR:</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I discovered (a few) exoplanet candidates, I walk through how. Then I talk about the near future of science given the ease of and growing capabilities of agentic systems. These are largely independent sections, you can skip ahead.</span></i></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LLM Usage:</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> All text was generated by a Human Language Model, the discovery itself was done mostly by Claude Opus 5.5. The widgets are all Claudeslop code but they really do help with the explanation so please bear with them.</span></i></p></blockquote><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exoplanet</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">T</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ransiting </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">xoplanet </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">S</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">urvey </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">S</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">atellite (</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transiting_Exoplanet_Survey_Satellite" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TESS</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) was built for detecting exoplanets. Each month (27 days) TESS focuses on a different patch of the sky, and every 200 seconds its wide field-of-view cameras repeat their observations of that particular patch of the celestial sphere. In 2023 </span><a href="https://www.nasa.gov/centers-and-facilities/goddard/nasas-tess-celebrates-fifth-year-scanning-the-sky-for-new-worlds" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NASA estimated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that TESS has photographed over 93% of the night sky at least once.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 60.31721869984422%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kdybMpcFJ5iwCDJsd/zpfqrdybcovtwb2ywhbt" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TESS's observational sectors, from NASA's </span><a href="https://heasarc.gsfc.nasa.gov/docs/tess/HowToFindAnExoplanet-UserVersion.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"How to find an Exoplanet with TESS data"</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In one of these sectors (55 to be exact</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref02bcfvo8esfo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn02bcfvo8esfo">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), there's a star elegantly named </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TIC 423560969.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Given that TESS is made to scan large swaths of the night sky very quickly, the resolution isn't too great, yet as you'll see there's still a pretty clear signal that can be extracted. </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can make a time-lapse of the month during which the star was recorded by TESS. It's not particularly illuminating. There's a lot of noise and being 2,300 light-years away doesn't help. It's not perceptible when a transit (partial eclipse) is happening. </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TESS actually scanned this star twice: Once in August 2022 and once in May 2025. By looking at when the star's brightness dips (or more precisely, at the period of the dips) we can get the exoplanet's orbital period. We can then test this on the other month of observations by TESS and double-check that the transit really does occur with that periodicity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are a few steps to cleanly extract the transit's effect on the star's brightness, removing the sky's (changing w.r.t time) background glow, de-noising etc.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given that TESS observed this star in two different months, years apart, we can actually nail down the period of the planet's orbit very precisely. You can see just how sensitive and clean of a fit this is here. We likely have the period down to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a second.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now let's take a look at all the transits recorded by TESS of this star, line them up according to our orbital period and average them out to get a more precise estimator of the star's "actual" drop in brightness if we had more sensitive instruments. </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, what information can we glean from this?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, the size of the planet determines how much light is occluded from our perspective, so that corresponds to the "Depth" of the drop in brightness. The plane in which the planet orbits also determines the drop in brightness (since the edges are dimmer than the center of the star, a phenomenon called </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limb_darkening" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">limb-darkening</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) but also how quickly the brightness drops and rises.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An exact analytical solution of the function of brightness over time parameterized by the planet's radius, offset and orbital radius is left as an exercise to the reader (the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/astro-ph/0210099" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">solution</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is rather abstruse). A simulation (which uses that solution to construct the best fit line) lets us see the general picture. </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now to be exact, this is an exoplanet </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">candidate</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, it could also be a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_dwarf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">brown dwarf</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binary_star#Eclipsing_binaries" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eclipsing star binary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or an eclipsing binary in the background (sharing those same 4 pixels). From the data I have now, without follow-up observations by a different telescope, I'm unable to distinguish between these four possibilities.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But of course I didn't discover one exoplanet candidate but rather (as of writing) 25 candidates. Previous transit-based methods had a </span><a href="https://www.universetoday.com/articles/5000-exoplanets" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">20.6% final outcome</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of being classified as exoplanets (and even more yet undecided), which implies that with a quick application of the binomial distribution, I've almost certainly discovered at least one exoplanet, even if it's not in </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TIC 423560969.</span></b></p><p><br /></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For completeness, here are all the stars in which I found candidates (</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">skippable</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are candidates that I'm pretty sure about and where the signal is clear:</span></p><table novel exoplanet" in Claude Code. I had to send messages (19 in this session) correcting and guiding Claude to focus on its goal and not to simply recreate other people's work, mostly in the vein of:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Opus 5.5: "I've found a new candidate! TIC 5[...]"</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to which I then had to ask again whether it had checked it with the database-checking script it wrote. Or when Claude decided to search specifically where other groups had already looked, to which I responded that that's likely not the best approach, but I'm no astrophysicist.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It took me around 5 hours to get results I was happy with, it didn't even eat up my usage limits for that session. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Still, getting science done was trivially easy and will become even more painless with time. This is one minor discovery which will most likely never be cited or used, but automated science is coming.</span></p><p><br /></p><hr /><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apocalypse</span></b></h3><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">late 14c., "revelation, disclosure," from Church Latin </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">apocalypsis</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "revelation," from Greek </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">apokalyptein</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "uncover, disclose, reveal," from </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">apo</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "off, away from" (see </span><a href="https://www.etymonline.com/word/apo-"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">apo-</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) + </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">kalyptein</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "to cover, conceal" (from PIE root </span><a href="https://www.etymonline.com/word/*kel-#etymonline_v_52556" rel="nofollow"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">*kel-</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (1) "to cover, conceal, save").</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-</span><a href="https://www.etymonline.com" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">etymonline</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></i><a href="https://www.etymonline.com/word/apocalypse" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">apocalypse</span></i></a></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">so</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> much raw data.</span></p><p><a href="https://maps.biogeochemical-argo.com/bgcargo/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Enmeshing our oceans</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are a fleet of 4,433 buoys; every 10 days they descend 2 kilometers recording the temperature and conductivity</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrdb4cbd1mbm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrdb4cbd1mbm">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Embedded in the Earth are more than 3,300 </span><a href="https://ismn.earth/en/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ISMN</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> stations measuring soil moisture and soil temperature</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjy7jqk98gv9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjy7jqk98gv9">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_Meteorological_Data_Relay" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Surveying the air</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are more than 4,000 commercial and cargo aircraft reporting temperature and pressure readings.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc5mk6zg9s89"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc5mk6zg9s89">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And of course, looking at the stars are many observatory </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Cumbres_Observatory" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">networks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">were</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> very few people looking at it. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There will soon be thousands of agents looking at all publicly available datasets, looking for anomalies. I know this because </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> going to instruct them to do so very soon, although I'm sure even right now other people are running agents to look at...everything and "find something"</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0okobcceg62e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0okobcceg62e">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond that, agents can download, parse, deduplicate, clean and analyze data at a speed that makes you forget that just a few years ago a </span><a href="https://www.computerworld.com/article/1639623/the-data-science-ecosystem-part-2-data-wrangling.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">common saying</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was that "Data scientists spend 80% of their time converting data into a usable form".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And beyond-beyond that, people were already setting up </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06792-0" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">automated laboratories </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in the GPT-4 days which could read papers, decide on which experiments to run and even run those experiments themselves through robotic actuators.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 58.91238670694864%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kdybMpcFJ5iwCDJsd/ggr89bjgch7m3s1fw2vu" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A mobile robotic chemist, </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-020-2442-2" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nature</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This of course results in more data which, if done right, is specifically data which we weren't sure about from in silico methods or previous results. The limiting factor becomes either analysis or physical experimentation</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given that LLM generation speeds have </span><a href="https://artificialanalysis.ai/trends?intelligence-range=gte-20%2Cgte-30%2Cgte-40%2Cgte-50%2Cgte-60#language-model-output-speed-by-intelligence-index-threshold-over-time" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gone up over time</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and Cerebras offers even higher speeds</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefskawpg05p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnskawpg05p">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I think it's very unlikely that analysis time becomes the bottleneck. Experimentation could take hours or even days for some protocols, but it still could be massively parallelized with many such autonomous robots doing different tasks for different experiments.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given that you can arbitrarily scale this setup to as many robots doing as many parallel autonomous experiments as you want (or can afford), and that doing this would be very profitable, we should expect a deluge of research being done imminently. </span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's already a deluge of slop papers, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/stats/monthly_submissions" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ArXiv monthly submissions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have been growing explosively.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And largely for the worse of it, reading new papers nowadays has been grating to say the least. One of the most underrated properties of humans is that (for the most part) they are lazy, and so, when writing a paper they will (for the most part) attempt to quickly and concisely write down their thoughts and central novel contributions. LLMs (most likely due to RLHF </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.03716" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rewarding increasing response length</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and thorough-looking padded responses) are horribly verbose by default and will invent useless new terminology just for the fun of it.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.besthub.dev/articles/iclr-2027-registrations-hit-60k-exceeding-all-prior-years-combined-6381ce5e8693" rel="noreferrer"></a><a href="https://www.besthub.dev/articles/iclr-2027-registrations-hit-60k-exceeding-all-prior-years-combined-6381ce5e8693" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ICLR</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 2027</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> received more than 60,000 abstract submissions (which of course is more than every previous year combined). These have been described as slop of the highest degree by many submission reviewers on Twitter:</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">selected tweets (</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">skippable</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><a href="https://x.com/banburismus_/status/2111626167602266144" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exhibit A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "claudeslop updates against RSI"</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><a href="https://x.com/gneubig/status/2111894872181375284" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exhibit B</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "LLM writing </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almost_everywhere" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">almost everywhere</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><a href="https://x.com/hjy836/status/2112058937285722261" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exhibit C</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "Even in school essays I cited more than 3 things"</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><a href="https://x.com/iamleonli/status/2111185308511592530" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exhibit D</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "slop reporting about slop (meta slop)" (Also 53% ChatGPT and 35% Codex?!)</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the writing of LLMs stays so illegible, the science Apocalypse will be decidedly less apocalyptic (to humans at least). Rather than an explosion of human knowledge across all domains of science, it will be nothing more than a </span><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/slopocalypse" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">slopocalypse</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, relegating all manner of exciting discoveries behind an (im-well, depends how much effort you put in)penetrable wall of confusion.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thankfully, I strongly believe that LLM writing being so bad is transitory. </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The horrifying statistic in the </span><a href="https://x.com/iamleonli/status/2111185308511592530" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">last tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> , that 53% of the AI tools named in ICLR AI Use statements were ChatGPT/GPT (with Codex counted separately), explains a lot. And this is for ICLR submissions! The slop pollution on the internet is driven by price: the cheapest (freest) tools make do. I think it's easy to sometimes forget inside the bubble that most AI content on the internet over the past few years has been made with the free tier on ChatGPT's web interface, rather than the sophisticated Claudish we've come to know.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models' writing has been improving recently. The jump from Opus 5 to Opus 5.5 directly targeted LLM writing and largely improved it. ICLR submissions were during the "dark ages" of Claude writing (4.8-5 by vague Twitter consensus).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's no reason to expect models to plateau before vastly superhuman communication. The benchmark here is pretty clear (how often can a model explain something to a human/proxy-model successfully) and I expect labs to hill climb on it imminently.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As Anthropic mentioned in the </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/claude-opus-5-5" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus 5.5 release</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, clear communication has both "practical and safety benefits". If the papers are totally incomprehensible to humans, this is obviously bad both for advancing human understanding and for inadvertently publishing </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/information-hazards" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">infohazards</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scientific writing wasn't widely regarded as clear even before LLMs (here's a </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KY4v594UoCo" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">good video</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about it), but it's still readable enough for other researchers to read and work off of. As of now the quality of academic writing has been dropping sharply, but that may change shortly for the reasons above.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During the writing of this post I discovered a very </span><a href="https://github.com/eumemic/exoplanets" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">similar project</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by user </span><a href="https://github.com/eumemic" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eumemic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They actually took a different approach from my straightforward search: they targeted stars on the border of two sectors, which were usually skipped due to the schlep of combining the sectors and dealing with them correctly. You can (and should) </span><a href="https://eumemic.github.io/exoplanets/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">read it here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. However, the author (Claude?) has a pretty poor theory of mind for the reader, suffering from the writing issues talked about above.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it's still a great project which really did find many (as of writing, 645) exoplanet candidates! And it's not bad science either: considerably more rigorous, well sourced and open about its methodology than a lot of published research. But with all commendations, it's a slog to read through!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1mb7rj8ui4e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1mb7rj8ui4e">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I hope the coming era of well-done science is also well communicated. </span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What about </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffusion_(business)" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">diffusion</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? Here I mean: how quickly will these scientific discoveries be adopted in our lives? If you want to accurately model the future (for example, modeling people's attitudes towards AI which are parameterized by inventions impacting their life) you need to understand both how much science will be done and how much of it will be used for inventions in people's daily life.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't have a calibrated answer for the second question. </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isomorphic_Labs" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Isomorphic labs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">' mission statement is "solve all disease" and I find it likely they partially succeed in the near future. Anthropic's goal of "cure all disease" with their wet lab research is also likely to be impactful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yet clinical trials take years. So it's very unclear how big of an impact they can make in the short term. I have seen very few discussions</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref100eox0uzruo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn100eox0uzruo">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the same bottleneck in other scientific fields (material science, chemistry etc.) and I don't have particularly good priors. Just as you can run a fleet of agents to do more science, you can run a fleet of agents to "look at the latest scientific papers, propose and test products you can sell to people to maximize profit". This of course is very profitable and is likely to be done at scale.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm also not sure new science is even necessary for fast diffusion; there's likely a "science overhang" today, where there are a lot of ideas and experiments done in academia which haven't yet been done commercially even though they likely are profitable. </span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A well-known story tells of a Žfinance professor and a student who come across a $100 bill lying on the ground. As the student stops to pick it up, the professor says,</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Don’t bother—if it were really a $100 bill, it wouldn’t be there.”</span></p><p><a href="https://eml.berkeley.edu/~craine/EconH195/Fall_14/webpage/Malkiel_Efficient%20Mkts.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The efficient market hypothesis and Its Critics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Burton G. Malkiel </span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Contrary to the efficient market hypothesis, I think that due to the small number of people doing and commercializing science, there really were nearly risk-free opportunities (consider my discovery just now, hiding in plain sight). I don't think these circumstances will persist when faced with the coming research swarms.</span></p><hr /><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What should you do about this?</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm really not sure. If you were ever interested in an area of science you had little formal training in, it's now trivially easy to make real (though incremental) advancements in it. I would suggest you at least try to do so to understand what's going to happen.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prompt your favorite agent with something like this</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvmn16o9t95g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvmn16o9t95g">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, change it to focus on a field in which you could at least read </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Glossaries_of_science" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wikipedia's glossary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of it (since you almost certainly need to "interject" during the agent's work).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course the most potentially beneficial aspect of this from an AI safety perspective is distributed, large-scale automated alignment and interpretability advancing the field years ahead at a time when it's desperately needed. The most harmful aspect of this from an AI safety perspective is distributed, large-scale pollution of science, forcing researchers to rely on unreliable systems, incorrect results and incomprehensible research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post covers the wider scope of "Science" but in my mind the most important subset is that of automated AI safety research. Making this flood of research genuinely helpful to advancing alignment is one of the most neglected and high-impact areas of work I can see right now. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jason Fantl </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RNMBaECHaCGfDfri8/jason-fantl-s-shortform?commentId=KBpRLjCEo3CRcG2s7" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recently posted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We may soon have a need to de-duplicate safety research. I recently spoke with the CEO of an AI safety nonprofit, and he said that a large part of his work is coordinating with others orgs to ensure he isn't accidentally working on the same project. Oftentimes you don't hear about work until it is finished, so it is very easy for work to be partially duplicated when lots of people are working in a similar area. I expect we will see am amplified version of this problem with autonomous researchers. Imagine a Claude in every lab, all looking at the same public research, and all deciding on the same "most promising" research direction. We will need some coordination mechanism once autonomous AI researchers are picking their own research directions.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This problem of course exists in all fields of science and will soon become extremely relevant. In the early modern era, the system of scientific deconfliction was implicit tacit knowledge, the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Letters" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Republic of Letters</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and royal institutions. The people doing novel science were a small number of mostly rich nobles who were in a small cohesive social group. This resulted in many </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_priority#Priority_disputes" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">priority disputes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> over who discovered what first</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftom5wazc3d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntom5wazc3d">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. To adapt to the growing amount of science being done, the academic journal system was invented, allowing more efficient retrieval of existing work and a method of staying up to date on the latest in a field. When even more people started doing science (and the technology available allowed it), arXiv became the central place to publish science, allowing searching and immediate publishing (as compared to the journal system).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How can we adapt to even more "people" doing science? I'm not sure, but I have a few not well fleshed out ideas:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Real-time viewing of work being done. For an agent, this could be as simple as showing a 3-line summary.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A way to immediately request group "merging" by trading relevant intermediate results and eventually publishing jointly.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An LLM-based retrieval system (maybe RAG?) to verify that the research you're proposing hasn't already been done.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A paper rating system composed of LLM judges on different metrics.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe also for "compute grant proposals" distributed to agents proposing research rated by judges as promising.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think nearly all of these have been done in isolation (I think </span><a href="https://www.alphaxiv.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AlphaXiv</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a good example) but there's not yet a widely adopted system that really feels like it will scale to the amount of automated science that will be done.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you want the science apocalypse to be comprehensible, beneficial to humanity, full of high-quality science and exciting, I think this is one of the most obvious ways to help.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn02bcfvo8esfo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref02bcfvo8esfo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technically 55 refers to the spacecraft orientation at the time, so in the earlier observations (5 Aug to 1 Sep 2022) it was sector 55 and in the later ones (7 May to 3 Jun 2025) it was sector 92.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrdb4cbd1mbm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrdb4cbd1mbm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And on </span><a href="https://argo.ucsd.edu/expansion/biogeochemical-argo-mission/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some floats</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: oxygen levels, Chlorophyll-a fluorescence, BBP, Nitrate, pH, irradiance, radiometers, cameras and more.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kdybMpcFJ5iwCDJsd/eotfawrrwggnqexkoc5j" /><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjy7jqk98gv9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjy7jqk98gv9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://ismn.earth/en/data/data-availability/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> precipitation, soil suction, snow depth, air temperature, surface temperature </span></p><img alt="" ## Engineering

### [Fragments: October 21](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-21.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Gergely Orosz is frustrated at those that entered his explicitly “No AI” essay competition and used AI. He <a href="https://x.com/GergelyOrosz/status/2108863325756961274">goes on to say:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>When I start to suspect some part is AI about someone I don’t know: how can I tell how much of it is? And how much time and effort do I want to invest to figure out?</p>

  <p>I fear most people do not realize this last part: that when it takes time and effort from others to figure out what % is AI, and what % is YOU, then I will simply assume it’s probably 100% AI and 0% you</p>

  <p>So when producing anything that is meant for other humans, if you use even a little AI, you risk the assumption that it is all AI and pointless to pay attention to</p>
</blockquote>

<p>In general, I agree with him, and react much the same. Once I hear LLM Voice, I flip the bozo bit and check out of the post.</p>

<p>But as I do this I have caveats. How accurate is my neural net at AI-detection, are humans taking on AI-ish elements in their normal communication? And more seriously there are some valid uses for AI. Jim Highsmith’s story of <a href="https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/60/9/677">his second brain helping him deal with Parkinson’s </a>is one. Another is folks who don’t know English well, using AI to translate. Am I being unfair when I flip the AI voice bit when I read/listen to them?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I was sad to hear that the <a href="https://anitab.org/press-release">Grace Hopper Celebration is canceled</a> this year. It’s an event run by Anitab.org to promote women and non-binary technologists in the software industry. Many of my colleagues have been encouraged and inspired by going to this event, which makes a refreshing change to what is often a male-dominated world. It seems the current crusade against diversity initiatives has had a big impact on funding. We are still a long way from where we should be on gender diversity in our profession, and losing an event like this only makes things more difficult.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve always found Cindy Sridharan’s writing to be worth reading, and she’s broken a quiet period with a monster of an article: <a href="https://copyconstruct.medium.com/how-to-effectively-debug-when-you-no-longer-understand-the-code-bd3dcb2c5f7b">How to effectively debug when you no longer understand the code</a>. Since Medium estimates it as a 64 minute read, I didn’t jump in lightly, but settled down on a flight from Amsterdam to Stuttgart. I’m glad I did, because there’s lot of good material in there, and I’d struggle to find anything to cut.</p>

<p>In the article she uses three recent bug hunts to surface principles for how to best use an AI tool for debugging. Much of this is governed by this wise observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Agents are not humans. They’re far more capable than humans when it comes to many things, and we should be leaning into these strengths, rather than trying to get agents to replicate human patterns of problem solving which might be suboptimal in agentic contexts.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>One conclusion from her experiences is that the code often isn’t the best thing for agents to look at</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>having an agent start an investigation by first reading the code is an anti-pattern. In fact, reading the code first to formulate hypotheses about a bug should always be the very last resort of the agentic investigation process, not the starting point. An agent should first collect all available evidence about the bug, and only then formulate the hypotheses from the code that might best explain what’s being observed.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She distinguishes between a bottom tier of facts: raw data that agent can find about the behavior of the system - and an upper tier of hypotheses: inferences that the agents make based on that data.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Diagnosing a bug now becomes a problem of finding the hypothesis in the top tier that satisfies every fact from the bottom tier.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The agents need to be steered towards the hypotheses that are the likest cause of the bug and then subject these hypotheses to validation. She has a three-step process to do this. First she identifies the most important facts, the signals that might explain or identify the bug.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The factual tier has many data points. Not all of them are equally valuable. In fact, some of it might be active noise that doesn’t help an agent to be steered towards most promising hypothesis. The agent first needs to identify the data points in the factual tier that are actually related to the bug. Agents do this best when they are given a high-quality control</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A control is a behavior of the system that doesn’t exhibit the bug. This may be requests that trigger the bug compared to those that don’t, or different builds of the product, or the same build in different environments.</p>

<p>With signals highlighted, she then focuses on the hypotheses that are most promising, because they fit best with the signals. A hypothesis should satisfy all the facts, the most valuable signals are those that are only satisfied by a few hypotheses. A table of hypotheses and signals helps visualize the strength of the signals and the likelihood of the hypotheses.</p>

<p>The third step is then validating the promising hypotheses against every fact, treating each fact as constraint on the solution.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Across the three bugs chronicled in this post, my most valuable contributions weren’t knowledge of the code. They were picking a control, choosing a constraint, and rejecting a hypothesis that hadn’t been validated. The engineers who thrive in an agent-native world will be the ones who are good at designing such verification loops, whether or not they understand the code it verifies.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This directing has to remember the opening point that agents have different capabilities to humans.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A human wants to deal with less data. All abstractions we currently have (aggregation, visualizations etc) are designed to help humans deal with less data. An agent, however, needs more raw data; its strength lies in processing huge volumes of data at a rapid clip. Aggregation — the thing that makes a dashboard useful to a human — is often the thing that destroys the value of the signal for an agent</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Agents’ ability to consume lots of raw data also affects how we build systems. More logging gives agents more data to chew on.</p>

<p>And thus far, the human is still necessary to direct the agent</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In each of the three bugs chronicled in this post, the agent was more than capable of getting to the root cause by itself, but it didn’t do so. It didn’t set up a control in the “latency bug” until I asked it to</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While it’s a long post, it’s engagingly written and full of useful details. I hope my little summary encourages you to give it the read it deserves.</p>
